Private Rate-Double-Robust Inference
Máté Kormos, Aad van der Vaart

Abstract




We reconcile privacy protection and rate-double-robust inference. The privacy of individuals is protected by a local privacy mechanism: injecting noise into their sensitive data, revealing only the noisy data for inference. Hence, privacy protection hinders inference. In contrast, the inference of a target parameter is rate-double-robust when the large-sample bias of an estimator of the parameter is characterised by a trade-off between the estimation errors of two other, nuisance, parameters. Hence, rate-double-robustness facilitates inference. Our starting point of reconciliation is a class of rate-double-robust target parameters indexed linearly by an infinite-dimensional and nonlinearly by a low-dimensional regression. Among others, this includes causal parameters. To infer these targets privately, we show how suitable privacy mechanisms transfer the semiparametric properties of the sensitive-data model to the private setting. Rate-double-robustness is transferred, enabling locally-private, unbiased and semiparametrically efficient inference of our target parameters. Finally, we transform general nonparametric nuisance estimators into private ones, which inherit convergence properties of their nonprivate counterparts. For parametric nuisance models, we develop a private method-of-moments estimator and its large-sample inference theory.

1.  Introduction

Sensitive data of units in a sample are desirable to protect. This may be accomplished by a privacy mechanism, which disguises the sensitive data by deliberately injecting noise into them. Next, only the noisy — and not the sensitive — data are revealed, preserving the privacy of sampled units, but hindering inference.

This paper is concerned with the inference of a parameter
\chi(P_{VX})
of the distribution
P_{VX}
of the data
(V,X)
when
X
is privacy-protected. Hence, we wish to infer
\chi(P_{VX})
from the data
(V,Z)
, where
Z
is the noisy version of
X
disguised by a given privacy mechanism.

We guarantee privacy by local mechanisms: the noise is injected to the sensitive data of each unit. Then we consider inference under a fixed mechanism, employed uniformly for each unit. While some of our results hold for general local mechanisms, specialising the mechanisms yields more interesting results. Our specialised mechanisms leave the sensitive data intact with probability
\alpha
, and output pure noise with probability
1-\alpha
, guaranteeing total-variation privacy
(
6
)
. It is this
\alpha
-identity which enables inference. Under this specialisation,
X
can take values in any measurable space, such as metric spaces. Thus, these mechanisms are much more flexible than those using additive noise
(
18
)
.

We focus on the inference of parameters
\chi(P_{VX})
with a rate-double-robustness (or mixed-bias) property. A parameter has this property if the large-sample bias of an estimator thereof is characterised by the product of the estimation errors of two other parameters, which are then called nuisance parameters. The product is attractive as the errors can compensate each other. This is favourable for infinite-dimensional nuisance parameters with large estimation errors. If the product vanishes,
\chi(P_{VX})
can be inferred unbiasedly in the large-sample limit. Examples of rate-double-robust parameters include average treatment effects.

Our contribution is threefold. First, we propose a novel class of rate-double-robust parameters in the
nonprivate
setting, motivated by
41
and
13
. The class comprises parameters which depend linearly on an infinite-dimensional regression and nonlinearly on a low-dimensional regression.
While
41
consider dependence parameters more general than regressions, they only allow for linear dependencies.
1
1

1

Their sufficient conditions for a product-form bias
(
41
, Proposition 3)
stipulate a parameter structure where both factors in the product are ratios of two regressions, with the same denominator in both. In general, the variationally dependent denominators do not naturally translate into double-robustness. An exception is when the denominator and the nominator are chosen so that the resulting ratio in each factor is itself a regression function. But then these parameters are strictly included in our class.
While
13
show that the average treatment effect on the treated — which falls into our class — is rate-double-robust, they do not generalise this result to nonlinear dependencies on low-dimensional regressions. Generalisation is straightforward as low-dimensional parameters are estimable at a fast rate.

Second, turning to the private setting, we provide conditions for the privacy mechanism to infer
\chi(P_{VX})
from the observed noisy data
(V,Z)\sim P_{VZ}
, and show how our specialised mechanisms satisfy them.
Namely, we connect the semiparametric properties of the statistical models for
P_{VX}
and
P_{VZ}
. Under our specialised mechanisms, if a parameter is rate-double-robust in the nonprivate setting, then so it remains in the private setting. This leads to privacy-protected large-sample unbiased inference of
\chi(P_{VX})
from
(V,Z)
, for fast-enough nuisance estimators. The limiting variance increases with the noise level of the privacy mechanism, but it is semiparametrically efficient in the private model induced by a nonparametric model for
P_{VX}
and by the specialised mechanism.

Third, we study private estimation of the nuisance parameters. Expressing them as expected-loss minimisers in the
private
setting paves the way for estimation through empirical risk minimisation. Alternatively, given a nonprivate “source” estimator in a general class of nonparametric estimators, we transform it to an estimator which uses only the noisy data
(V,Z)
. We show how the transformed estimator inherit the guarantees of its nonprivate source. For example, the convergence rates of privatised kernel and orthogonal series estimators remain the same as those of their nonprivate counterparts inflated by the noise level of the privacy mechanism. For parametric nuisance models, we develop a private method-of-moments estimator for
K
-valued parameters identified from moment conditions
(
23
;
33
)
. We also derive its limiting distribution — an apparently new result in private inference.

In summary, to the best of our knowledge, our work is the first achieving locally private, efficient and unbiased rate-double-robust inference for parameters as general as the ones in our proposed class, and with data taking values in generic spaces.

In
Section

2
, we situate our work in the literature. In
Section

3
, we introduce our rate-double-robust class without privacy.
Section

4
adds privacy.
Section

5
discusses private estimation of the double-robust parameters.
Section

6
focuses on the private estimation of nuisance parameters.
Section

7
concludes.

2.  Literature

Privacy-preserving inference
(
50
;
19
;
18
)
can offer central or local privacy guarantees; see
14
for a survey.
2
2

2

Some other privacy notions are
element-level privacy

(
4
)
or homomorphic encryption
(
21
;
51
)
.
Central mechanisms inject noise into sample aggregates, hence are less stringent than the local ones adopted by us, which noise individual data.

In the central paradigm, parametric (regression) models are studied by
45
,
43
,
3
,
24
, and the nonparametric median by
15
. In the local paradigm, convergence rates
(
31
;
1
;
9
)
and Fisher-information bounds
(
7
)
are derived.

Causal parameters are important instances in our class. Their private inference is addressed by
29
,
52
,
35
, and
2
. Compared to them, we support more general private covariate adjustment or parameters, under less stringent assumptions about
X
.

Private efficiency theory, also optimising over the privacy mechanism, is pioneered by
46
for parametric models. We only consider efficient inference for a
given
mechanism, but we adopt nonparametric models like
16
and
17
do. They provide minimax rates up to constants, whereas our results are asymptotically exact.
11
considers minimax rates in nonparametric models, specialised to the expected density; we infer parameters in a broad class.

Private M-estimators have been well studied for parametric models
(
12
;
26
;
8
;
20
;
30
;
44
;
32
)
. Yet, only
4
and
5
appear to derive limiting distributions. Compared to the former, we offer more stringent, local privacy; unlike the latter, our method-of-moments estimator is asymptotically unbiased.

3.  Rate-Double-Robust Inference

In this section, we construct our target parameters
\chi(P_{VX})
and derive their inferential properties
without
privacy. This serves as a basis for private inference in
Section

4
. Thus, for now, we consider the nonprivate setting when the sensitive data
(V,X)
are observable.

Here,
(V,X)
is a random element defined on the probability space
(\Omega,\mathscr{F}_{\Omega},\mathbb{P})
, with distribution
P_{VX}
belonging to
\mathcal{P}_{VX}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{\mathfrak{V}\mathfrak{X}}
, where
\mathcal{P}_{\mathfrak{V}\mathfrak{X}}
is the set of all possible distributions on the measurable space
(\mathfrak{V}\times\mathfrak{X},\mathscr{F}_{\mathfrak{V}\times\mathfrak{X}})
, where
\mathfrak{V}=\mathfrak{V}_{\mathfrak{1}}\times\mathfrak{V}_{\mathfrak{2}}\times\ldots
. Our primary interest is in the nonparametric model

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{VX}=\mathcal{P}_{\mathfrak{V}\mathfrak{X}}.

(M)

Each functional
\chi:\mathcal{P}_{VX}\to\real
yields a parameter
\chi(P_{VX})
.
Section

3.1
introduces our class of
\chi
which yield rate-double-robust parameters;
Section

3.2
studies their inferential properties.

Notation.
For
h:\mathfrak{V}\times\mathfrak{X}\to\real
, we let
P_{VX}h\coloneqq P_{VX}h(V,X)\coloneqq\int_{\mathfrak{V}\times\mathfrak{X}}h(v,x)\,\mathrm{d}P_{VX}(v,x)
. Fix
p\in[1,\infty)
. With
\left\lVert{h}\right\rVert_{L_{p}(P_{VX})}\coloneqq\left(P_{VX}|h|^{p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}
, write
L_{p}(P_{VX})
for all
h:\mathfrak{V}\times\mathfrak{X}\to\real
with
\left\lVert{h}\right\rVert_{L_{p}(P_{VX})}^{p}<\infty
, and
L_{p}^{0}(P_{VX})
for all
h\in L_{p}(P_{VX})
with
P_{VX}h=0
. Let
\left\lVert{h}\right\rVert_{\infty}\coloneqq\sup_{(v,x)\in\mathfrak{V}\times\mathfrak{X}}|h(v,x)|
, and
\rho((h,a),(h^{\prime},a^{\prime}))\coloneqq\left\lVert{h-h^{\prime}}\right\rVert_{L_{2}(P_{VX})}+|a-a^{\prime}|
be a metric on
L_{2}(P_{VX})\times\real\ni(h,a)
. Let
\delta_{(v,x)}
be the Dirac measure at
(v,x)\in\mathfrak{V}\times\mathfrak{X}
. We call a
(\pi_{1}\circ V,\pi_{2}\circ V,\ldots)
a collection of coordinates of
V
, if all the
\pi_{j}\circ v\in\mathfrak{V}_{\mathfrak{j^{\prime}}}
for all
v\in\mathfrak{V}
for some
\mathfrak{V}_{\mathfrak{j^{\prime}}}\in\{\mathfrak{V}_{\mathfrak{1}},\mathfrak{V}_{\mathfrak{2}},\ldots\}
; for example, if
\mathfrak{V}=\mathfrak{V}_{\mathfrak{1}}\times\mathfrak{V}_{\mathfrak{2}}\times\mathfrak{V}_{\mathfrak{3}}
with corresponding
V=(V_{1},V_{2},V_{3})
, then
(V_{2},V_{1})
is a collection of coordinates of
V
.

3.1.  Rate-Double-Robust Parameter Class

Let
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
be two arbitrary collections of coordinates of
V
with values in
\mathfrak{V}_{\mathit{1}}
and
\mathfrak{V}_{\mathit{2}}
, respectively, where, importantly,
\mathfrak{V}_{\mathit{2}}
is finite. For given
m,g:\mathfrak{V}\times\mathfrak{X}\to\real
, define the regressions

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mu_{\mathcal{X}}(v_{\mathit{1}},x)&\coloneqq\mathbb{E}\left[\left.m(V,X)\,\right|\,V_{\mathit{1}}=v_{\mathit{1}},X=x\right],\quad(v_{\mathit{1}},x)\in\mathfrak{V}_{\mathit{1}}\times\mathfrak{X},\\
\gamma_{\mathcal{V}}(v_{\mathit{2}})&\coloneqq\mathbb{E}\left[\left.g(V,X)\,\right|\,V_{\mathit{2}}=v_{\mathit{2}}\right],\quad v_{\mathit{2}}\in\mathfrak{V}_{\mathit{2}},\end{aligned}

(R)

assuming
\mu_{\mathcal{X}}\in L_{2}(P_{V_{\mathit{1}}X}),\gamma_{\mathcal{V}}\in L_{2}(P_{V_{\mathit{2}}})
. For a given
f:\mathfrak{V}\times\mathfrak{X}\times L_{2}(P_{V_{\mathit{1}}X})\times L_{2}(P_{V_{\mathit{2}}})\to\real
, our targets are
\chi(P_{VX})=\mathbb{E}f(V,X,\mu_{\mathcal{X}},\gamma_{\mathcal{V}})
. As
\mathfrak{V}_{\mathit{2}}
is finite, we can define without loss of generality our target parameter as

\displaystyle\chi(P_{VX})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathbb{E}f(V,X,\mu_{\mathcal{X}},\gamma_{\mathcal{V}}(c))

(T)

for a fixed
c\in\mathfrak{V}_{\mathit{2}}
, and
f:\mathfrak{V}\times\mathfrak{X}\times L_{2}(P_{V_{\mathit{1}}X})\times\Gamma\to\real
, for
\Gamma\supseteq g(\mathfrak{X},\mathfrak{V})
. We constrain
f
, requiring that

\displaystyle L_{2}(P_{V_{\mathit{1}}X})\ni\mu\mapsto\mathbb{E}f(V,X,\mu,\gamma)

\displaystyle\text{ be $\left\lVert{\cdot}\right\rVert_{L_{2}(P_{VX})}$-continuous for all }\gamma\in\Gamma,

(C.C)

\displaystyle L_{2}(P_{V_{\mathit{1}}X})\ni\mu\mapsto f(V,X,\mu,\gamma)

\displaystyle\text{ be linear }P_{VX}\text{-a.s. for all }\gamma\in\Gamma,

(C.L)

\displaystyle\Gamma\ni\gamma\mapsto f(V,X,\mu,\gamma)

\displaystyle\text{ be twice continuously differentiable }P_{VX}\text{-a.s. for all }

\displaystyle\text{ }\mu\in L_{2}(P_{V_{\mathit{1}}X})\text{ with }\text{derivatives $\partial_{\gamma}f$, $\partial_{\gamma}^{2}f$.}

(C.D)

Conditions (
C.C
), (
C.L
), and (
C.D
) restrict the structure of the dependencies on the regressions to enforce rate-double-robustness.

Further, we impose the integrability conditions

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathbb{E}f(V,X,\mu_{\mathcal{X}},\gamma_{\mathcal{V}}(c))^{2}<\infty,\quad\mathbb{E}\mathbbm{1}_{V_{\mathit{2}}=c}g(V,X)^{2}<\infty,\\
\mathbb{E}\left[\left.m(V,X)^{2}\,\right|\,V_{\mathit{1}},X\right]<\infty\quad\text{ $P_{V_{\mathit{1}}X}$-a.s..}\end{aligned}

(C.I)

Different choices of
(V_{\mathit{1}},V_{\mathit{2}},m,g,f)
satisfying the above conditions give rise to a class of parameters of the form (
T
). In
Section

B.1
, we present examples such as average treatment effects (
41
;
13
), “geometric parameters,” and parameters from economics. We also demonstrate how dependence on
multiple
regressions can be accommodated. In
Section

3.2
, we show how parameters in this class lend themselves to rate-double-robust inference.

3.2.  Inferential Properties

Consider the one-step estimator
(
10
;
49
;
25
)
of
\chi(P_{VX})
. Starting from an arbitrary “plug-in” estimator
\chi(\hat{P}_{VX})
constructed from a random sample
\mathcal{S}\coloneqq((V_{i},X_{i}))_{i\in[n]}
from
P_{VX}
, the one-step estimator

\displaystyle\hat{\chi}_{n}\coloneqq\chi(\hat{P}_{VX})+\mathbb{P}_{n}\hat{\tilde{\chi}}=\chi(\hat{P}_{VX})+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in[n]}\hat{\tilde{\chi}}(V_{i},X_{i})

(1)

corrects for the plug-in bias via a directional-derivative expansion of the functional
\chi

(
49
)
. This derivate is representable with the so-called efficient influence function
\tilde{\chi}
of
\chi(P_{VX})
for the model
\mathcal{P}_{VX}
. Hence, informally,

\displaystyle\chi(\hat{P}_{VX})-\chi(P_{VX})\approx-P_{VX}\hat{\tilde{\chi}},

(2)

expanding by the estimate
\hat{\tilde{\chi}}
of
\tilde{\chi}
in the direction
P_{VX}-\hat{P}_{VX}
. This motivates (
1
) with the unknown
P_{VX}\hat{\tilde{\chi}}
estimated with
\mathbb{P}_{n}\hat{\tilde{\chi}}
.

To construct
\hat{\tilde{\chi}}
in (
1
), we need
\tilde{\chi}
. Let
r\in L_{2}(P_{V_{\mathit{1}}X})
be the function satisfying

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}f(V,X,\mu,\gamma_{\mathcal{V}}(c))=\mathbb{E}r(V_{\mathit{1}},X)\mu(V_{\mathit{1}},X)\quad\text{ for all }\mu\in L_{2}(P_{V_{\mathit{1}}X}).

(3)

The existence and uniqueness of
r
follows from the Riesz representation theorem by (
C.C
) and (
C.L
), whence
r
is called the Riesz representer. The representer
r
, whose dependence on
(P_{VX},f,c)
is silent in our notation, is obtained by manipulating the left-hand side of (
3
) until the right-hand side is reached (see
Section

B.1
). With
r
,
\tilde{\chi}
is derived in
Proposition

1
. The dependence on the regressions manifests itself in (
4
); under no dependence, the (generalised) derivates
r,\partial_{\gamma}f
are zero.

Proposition 1

(Efficient Influence Function of
\chi(P_{VX})
)
.

In the nonparametric model (
M
) for
P_{VX}
, the efficient influence function
\tilde{\chi}:\mathfrak{V}\times\mathfrak{X}\to\real
of
\chi(P_{VX})
in (
T
) is, at
P_{VX}
,

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \tilde{\chi}(v,x)\coloneqq&\,r(v_{\mathit{1}},x)(m(v,x)-\mu_{\mathcal{X}}(v_{\mathit{1}},x))\\
&+\frac{\mathbbm{1}_{v_{\mathit{2}}=c}}{p_{V_{\mathit{2}}}(c)}(g(v,x)-\gamma_{\mathcal{V}}(c))\mathbb{E}\partial_{\gamma}f(V,X,\mu_{\mathcal{X}},\gamma_{\mathcal{V}}(c))\\
&+f(v,x,\mu_{\mathcal{X}},\gamma_{\mathcal{V}}(c))-\chi(P_{VX}),\quad(v,x)\in\mathfrak{X}\times\mathfrak{V},\end{aligned}

(4)

where we denote by
v_{\mathit{1}},v_{\mathit{2}}
the coordinates of
v
that correspond to
V_{\mathit{1}},V_{\mathit{2}}
of
V
. When
V_{\mathit{2}}=\varnothing
, it is understood that
\frac{\mathbbm{1}_{v_{\mathit{2}}=c}}{p_{V_{\mathit{2}}}(c)}(g(v,x)-\gamma_{\mathcal{V}}(c))=g(v,x)-\mathbb{E}g(V,X)
.

Proof.

All proofs are in the appendix.
∎

Now we verify that
\chi(P_{VX})
in (
T
) is rate-double-robust.
Theorem

1
below controls the approximation error in (
2
). Because
\mathfrak{V}_{\mathit{2}}
is finite, the first, product term dominates in (
6
) for reasonable “estimators”
p_{V_{\mathit{2}}}^{\prime}(c),\gamma_{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}(c)
. This proves the one-step estimator (
1
) of
\chi(P_{VX})
rate-double-robust with bias characterised by the product of estimation errors of the nuisance parameters
\mu_{\mathcal{X}},r
.

Theorem 1

(Rate-Double-Robustness)
.

Let
\mu_{\mathcal{X}}^{\prime},r^{\prime}\in L_{2}(P_{V_{\mathit{1}}X})
and
p_{V_{\mathit{2}}}^{\prime}(c),\gamma_{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}(c),\chi^{\prime},e^{\prime\prime}\in\real
all be arbitrary. Set

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \tilde{\chi}^{\prime}(v,x)\coloneqq&\,r^{\prime}(v_{\mathit{1}},x)(m(v,x)-\mu_{\mathcal{X}}^{\prime}(v_{\mathit{1}},x))+\frac{\mathbbm{1}_{v_{\mathit{2}}=c}}{p_{V_{\mathit{2}}}^{\prime}(c)}(g(v,x)-\gamma_{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}(c))e^{\prime\prime}\\
&+f(v,x,\mu_{\mathcal{X}}^{\prime},\gamma_{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}(c))-\chi^{\prime}.\end{aligned}

(5)

Then

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \chi^{\prime}-\chi(P_{VX})+P_{VX}\tilde{\chi}^{\prime}=&\,-P_{VX}(r-r^{\prime})(\mu_{\mathcal{X}}-\mu_{\mathcal{X}}^{\prime})\\
&+(\gamma_{\mathcal{V}}(c)-\gamma_{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}(c))\left(\frac{p_{V_{\mathit{2}}}(c)}{p_{V_{\mathit{2}}}^{\prime}(c)}e^{\prime\prime}-e^{\prime}\right)\\
&-(\gamma_{\mathcal{V}}(c)-\gamma_{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}(c))^{2}\frac{P_{VX}\partial_{\gamma}^{2}f(V,X,\mu_{\mathcal{X}}^{\prime},\widetilde{\gamma_{\mathcal{V}}(c)})}{2}\end{aligned}

(6)

for some
\widetilde{\gamma_{\mathcal{V}}(c)}
between
\gamma_{\mathcal{V}}(c)
and
\gamma_{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}(c)
, and
e^{\prime}\coloneqq P_{VX}\partial_{\gamma}f(V,X,\mu_{\mathcal{X}}^{\prime},\gamma_{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}(c))
.

The function
\tilde{\chi}
is a key object in semiparametric efficiency theory. By definition, it is an element of the
L_{2}(P_{VX})
-completion
\overline{\mathrm{lin}\,}\mathcal{T}_{VX}
of the linear span of the
tangent set of the model
\mathcal{P}_{VX}
at
P_{VX}
,
\mathcal{T}_{VX}
: the set of all
directions in which
P_{VX}
can be perturbed so that the perturbed
P_{VX}
remains in
\mathcal{P}_{VX}
. For the nonparametric model
\mathcal{P}_{\mathfrak{V}\mathfrak{X}}
in (
M
),
\overline{\mathrm{lin}\,}\mathcal{T}_{VX}=\mathcal{T}_{VX}=L_{2}^{0}(P_{VX})
. Among all elements of
\overline{\mathrm{lin}\,}\mathcal{T}_{VX}
, it is
\tilde{\chi}
whose squared norm
P_{VX}\tilde{\chi}^{2}
is the limiting variance of asymptotically efficient estimators of
\chi(P_{VX})
(
10
). In the nonprivate setting, we show that (
1
) is asymptotically efficient (
Appendix

E
). In the following, we prove an analogue in the private setting.

4.  Privacy

To preserve the privacy of each unit in the sample
\mathcal{S}=((V_{i},X_{i}))_{i\in[n]}
, a noisy version
Z_{i}
of
X_{i}
is generated, and only
\bar{\mathcal{S}}\coloneqq((V_{i},Z_{i}))_{i\in[n]}
is revealed to infer
\chi(P_{VX})
. In
Section

4.1
, we describe how
Z_{i}
is generated to enable inference from
\bar{\mathcal{S}}
, which is discussed in
Section

4.2
.

4.1.  Privacy Mechanism

The
Z_{i}
are generated from
X_{i}
via a privacy mechanism, which can be thought of as a random map. Given a measurable space
(\mathfrak{Z},\mathscr{F}_{\mathfrak{Z}})
, the
Z_{i}
are generated as random draws
Z_{i}{|\,}((V_{j},X_{j}))_{j\in[n]}\sim Q(\cdot{|\,}X_{i})
for all
i\in[n]
for a
Q\in\mathcal{Q}(\mathfrak{X}\to\mathfrak{Z})
, where
\mathcal{Q}(\mathfrak{X}\to\mathfrak{Z})
is the set of all Markov kernels
Q:\mathscr{F}_{\mathfrak{Z}}\times\mathfrak{X}\to[0,1]
, so that
B\mapsto Q(B{|\,}x)
is a probability measure for all
x\in\mathfrak{X}
, and
x\mapsto Q(B{|\,}x)
is measurable for every
B\in\mathscr{F}_{\mathfrak{Z}}
. The kernel
Q
is called a
local noninteractive privacy mechanism
: local, because it noises the data of each unit
i
, and noninteractive, because it does not use the data of units
j\neq i
to generate
Z_{i}

(
46
)
. Therefore,
((V_{i},Z_{i}))_{i\in[n]}
is a random sample from the distribution of
(V,Z)
, the mixture

\displaystyle P_{VZ}(B_{\mathsf{v}},B_{\mathsf{z}})=\int_{B_{\mathsf{v}}}\int_{\mathfrak{X}}Q(B_{\mathsf{z}}{|\,}x)\,\mathrm{d}P_{VX}(v,x),\quad B_{\mathsf{v}}\in\mathscr{F}_{\mathfrak{V}},B_{\mathsf{z}}\in\mathscr{F}_{\mathfrak{Z}}.

(7)

The distribution
P_{VZ}
, determined by
Q
and
P_{VX}
, thus belongs to the model

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{VZ}(Q,\mathcal{P})\coloneqq\left\{P\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathfrak{V}\mathfrak{Z}}:P(B_{\mathsf{v}},B_{\mathsf{z}})=\int_{B_{\mathsf{v}}}\int_{\mathfrak{X}}Q(B_{\mathsf{z}}{|\,}x)\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{P}(v,x)\right.

\displaystyle\text{ holds for all }\left.B_{\mathsf{v}}\in\mathscr{F}_{\mathfrak{V}},B_{\mathsf{z}}\in\mathscr{F}_{\mathfrak{Z}},\text{ as }\tilde{P}\text{ runs through }\mathcal{P}\subset\mathcal{P}_{\mathfrak{V}\mathfrak{X}}\vphantom{\int_{B_{\mathsf{v}}}\int_{\mathfrak{X}}}\right\},

(8)

the set of all possible distributions of
(V,Z)
induced by the mechanism
Q
as the distribution of
(V,X)
varies across
\mathcal{P}
; here,
\mathcal{P}_{\mathfrak{V}\mathfrak{Z}}
is the set of all probability distributions on
(\mathfrak{V}\times\mathfrak{Z},\mathscr{F}_{\mathfrak{V}\times\mathfrak{Z}})
. Fixing
\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{P}_{VX}
and
\tilde{P}=P_{VX}
in (
8
) yields
P=P_{VZ}
.

How to choose the mechanism? First, the output space
\mathfrak{Z}
has to be specified. The choice of
\mathfrak{Z}
is an unexplored topic in privacy literature, beyond our current scope. Hence, for some of our results to follow,
\mathfrak{Z}
can be any given measurable space; however,
\mathfrak{Z}=\mathfrak{X}
— the usual choice in the literature — shall yield more insightful results.

Second, given
\mathfrak{Z}
, a mechanism
Q\in\mathcal{Q}(\mathfrak{X}\to\mathfrak{Z})
has to be chosen. Regard (
7
) as the flow of information from
P_{VX}
to
P_{VZ}
. If
Q(\cdot{|\,}x)
does not depend on
x
, then
Z
carries no information about
X
, constituting maximal privacy but precluding inference. In the other extreme, if
\mathfrak{Z}=\mathfrak{X}
, and
Q(\cdot{|\,}x)=\delta_{x}
concentrates on
X
, then
P_{VZ}=P_{VX}
, leading to the opposite effect. Thus, a sufficient and necessary condition for the identification of
every
parameter
\chi(P_{VX})
from
P_{VZ}
is the existence of a map
L_{Q}:\mathcal{P}_{VZ}(Q,\mathcal{P}_{VX})\to\mathcal{P}_{VX}
such that

\displaystyle P_{VX}=L_{Q}(P_{VZ}).

(9)

The map
L_{Q}
inverts (
7
) to recover
P_{VX}
from every
P_{VZ}
generated by a given
Q
. With
L_{Q}
, every parameter
3
3

3

The existence of an
L_{Q}
satisfying (
9
) is not necessary (but clearly sufficient) for the identification of some parameters — e.g. when
\chi
is the functional of only the marginal
P_{V}
.
of the sensitive-data distribution is identifiable from the noisy-data distribution as

\displaystyle\psi(P_{VZ})\coloneqq\chi\circ L_{Q}(P_{VZ})=\chi(P_{VX}).

(10)

The dependence of
\psi
on
Q
remains implicit in our notation. To infer
\chi(P_{VX})
, we wish to choose
Q
such that
L_{Q}
exists. Consider first a discrete
X
.

Example 1

(Finitely discretely distributed
X
)
.

Suppose that
\mathfrak{X}=\left\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{|\mathfrak{X}|}\right\}
and
\mathfrak{Z}=\left\{z_{1},\ldots,z_{|\mathfrak{Z}|}\right\}
are finite sets, and that
P_{VX}
has a
\nu_{V}\times\nu_{X}
-density
p_{VX}
for the counting measure
\nu_{X}
.
Then
P_{VZ}
in (
7
) admits a
\nu_{V}\times\nu_{X}
-density

\displaystyle p_{VZ}(v,z)=\sum_{x\in\mathfrak{X}}Q(\left\{z\right\}{|\,}x)p_{VX}(v,x),\quad(v,z)\in\mathfrak{V}\times\mathfrak{Z}.

(11)

Representing
Q
as the
|\mathfrak{Z}|
-by-
|\mathfrak{X}|
matrix

\displaystyle Q=\begin{bmatrix}(Q(\left\{z_{j}\right\}\mid x_{1}))_{j\in[|\mathfrak{Z}|]}&(Q(\left\{z_{j}\right\}\mid x_{2}))_{j\in[|\mathfrak{Z}|]}&\cdots&(Q(\left\{z_{j}\right\}\mid x_{|\mathfrak{X}|}))_{j\in[|\mathfrak{Z}|]}\end{bmatrix},

(12)

the display (
11
) is equivalent to

\displaystyle[0,1]^{|\mathfrak{Z}|\times 1}\ni\bar{p}_{VZ}(v)\coloneqq(p_{VZ}(v,z_{j}))_{j\in[|\mathfrak{Z}|]}=Q(p_{VX}(v,x_{j}))_{j\in[{|\mathfrak{X}|}]}\eqqcolon Q\bar{p}_{VX}(v),\quad v\in\mathfrak{V}.

For
z\in\real^{|\mathfrak{Z}|\times 1},x\in\real^{|\mathfrak{X}|\times 1}
and
Q\in\mathcal{Q}(\left\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{|\mathfrak{X}|}\right\}\to\left\{z_{1},\ldots,z_{|\mathfrak{Z}|}\right\})
, consider the system of linear equations

z=Qx
in
x
. Only if
|\mathfrak{Z}|\geq|\mathfrak{X}|
, can this system have a unique solution. Let us impose
|\mathfrak{Z}|\coloneqq|\mathfrak{X}|\eqqcolon J
, and set

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{J}\coloneqq\mathcal{Q}(\left\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{J}\right\}\to\left\{z_{1},\ldots,z_{J}\right\}).

(13)

Then the system has a unique solution for all
z\in\real^{J\times 1}
if and only if
Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{J}
viewed as a matrix is invertible with inverse
Q^{-1}
, in which case the solution is
Q^{-1}z
(e.g.
36
). Conclude that if
Q
is invertible, then
\bar{p}_{VX}(v)=Q^{-1}\bar{p}_{VZ}(v)
for all
v\in\mathfrak{V}
. Hence, if

\displaystyle Q\in\mathcal{Q}^{\mathrm{I}}_{J}\coloneqq\left\{Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{J}:Q^{-1}\text{ exists}\right\}

(14)

then
L_{Q}
in (
9
) exists, is unique, and is completely determined by the matrix
Q^{-1}
. An example (
46
) of
Q\in\mathcal{Q}^{\mathrm{I}}_{J}
is

\displaystyle Q=c_{J,\alpha}\begin{bmatrix}e^{\alpha}&1&\cdots&1\\
1&e^{\alpha}&\cdots&1\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
1&1&\cdots&e^{\alpha}\end{bmatrix},Q^{-1}=c_{J,\alpha}^{(-1)}\begin{bmatrix}e^{\alpha}+J-2&-1&\cdots&-1\\
-1&e^{\alpha}+J-2&\cdots&-1\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
-1&-1&\cdots&e^{\alpha}+J-2\end{bmatrix}

(15)

with
c_{J,\alpha}\coloneqq\frac{1}{e^{\alpha}+J-1}
and
c_{J,\alpha}^{(-1)}\coloneqq\frac{e^{\alpha}+J-1}{e^{2\alpha}+(J-2)e^{\alpha}-J+1}
for any
\alpha>0
.

Example

1
shows in (
15
) the role of the parameter
\alpha
determining the privacy level: an
\alpha\approx 0
equalises the entries of
Q
, with no information flowing from
X
to
Z
. Formally,
Q
in (
15
) satisfies

(e^{\alpha}-1)
-total-variational privacy for any
0<\alpha\leq\log(2)
:

Definition 1

(Local
\alpha
-Total-Variation Privacy (
\alpha
-LTVP)
6
)
.

For
0\leq\alpha\leq 1
, a mechanism
Q\in\mathcal{Q}(\mathfrak{X}\to\mathfrak{Z})
is locally
\alpha
-total-variationally private if
\sup_{B\in\mathscr{F}_{\mathfrak{Z}}}|Q(B{|\,}x)-Q(B{|\,}x^{\prime})|\leq\alpha
for all
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{X}
.

For generic
X
as well, total-variational privacy proves to be a suitable paradigm to ensure the existence of
L_{Q}
. Consider

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{\delta}\coloneqq\left\{Q\in\mathcal{Q}(\mathfrak{X}\to\mathfrak{X}):Q(B{|\,}x)=\alpha\delta_{x}(B)+(1-\alpha)\bar{Q}(B),\alpha\in(0,1),\bar{Q}\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathfrak{X}}\right\},

(16)

where
\mathcal{P}_{\mathfrak{X}}
is the set of all probability measures on
(\mathfrak{X},\mathscr{F}_{\mathfrak{X}})
. The
Z
drawn from a mechanism in (
16
) for a unit with
X=x
equals
x
itself with probability
\alpha
, and is pure noise drawn from
\bar{Q}
with probability
1-\alpha
. Hence, the smaller
\alpha
, the stricter the privacy. Trivially, any
Q
in (
16
) is
\alpha
-LTVP, and, by
Lemma

4
in
Section

C.3
, it ensures the existence of

\displaystyle(L_{Q}P_{VZ})(B_{\mathsf{v}},B_{\mathsf{x}})\coloneqq\frac{1}{\alpha}P_{VZ}(B_{\mathsf{v}},B_{\mathsf{x}})-\frac{1-\alpha}{\alpha}P_{V}(B_{\mathsf{v}})\bar{Q}(B_{\mathsf{x}})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\alpha}P_{VZ}(B_{\mathsf{v}},B_{\mathsf{x}})-\frac{1-\alpha}{\alpha}P_{VZ}(B_{\mathsf{v}},\mathfrak{X})\bar{Q}(B_{\mathsf{x}})=P_{VX}(B_{\mathsf{v}},B_{\mathsf{x}}),\quad B_{\mathsf{v}}\in\mathscr{F}_{\mathfrak{V}},B_{\mathsf{x}}\in\mathscr{F}_{\mathfrak{X}},

a linear map, whereby the identification (
10
) of
\chi(P_{VX})
from
P_{VZ}
readily follows.

It may seem restrictive that for discrete
X
we allow for any invertible mechanism, but for generic
X
we confine ourselves to (
16
). However, for generic
X
, it appears difficult to obtain
L_{Q}
without a Dirac measure; see
Section

C.5
for further discussion. In summary, we collect in

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{\psi}\coloneqq\mathcal{Q}^{\mathrm{I}}_{J}\cup\mathcal{Q}_{\delta}

(17)

the set of mechanisms implying (
10
), understanding that
Q\in\mathcal{Q}^{\mathrm{I}}_{J}
only if
\mathfrak{X}
and
P_{VX}
are as in
Example

1
.

4.2.  Private Inferential Properties

Now we derive the private analogue of the inferential properties in
Section

3.2
. This entails the efficient influence function of
\psi(P_{VZ})
in (
10
) at
P_{VZ}
in the model
\mathcal{P}_{VZ}(Q,\mathcal{P}_{VX})
of (
8
),
given
a mechanism
Q\in\mathcal{Q}(\mathfrak{X}\to\mathfrak{Z})
.
4
4

4

See
Lemma

1
in
Section

C.1
for further semiparametric properties.
In line with private-inference practice,
Q
is treated as common knowledge available for inference. Some of our results hold for any model
\mathcal{P}_{VX}
, but the main interest is in the nonparametric model
\mathcal{P}_{\mathfrak{V}\mathfrak{X}}
of (
M
).

For
Q\in\mathcal{Q}(\mathfrak{X}\to\mathfrak{Z})
, define the linear operator
Q_{\mathcal{X}}:L_{2}(P_{VZ})\to L_{2}(P_{VX})
as

\displaystyle(Q_{\mathcal{X}}k)(v,x)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\int_{\mathfrak{Z}}k(v,z)Q(\mathrm{d}z{|\,}x)=\mathbb{E}\left[\left.k(V,Z)\,\right|\,V=v,X=x\right],

(18)

whose properties are derived in
Lemma

3
in
Section

C.3
. In particular, it has adjoint
Q_{\mathcal{X}}^{*}:L_{2}(P_{VX})\to L_{2}(P_{VZ}),h\mapsto\mathbb{E}\left[\left.h(V,X)\,\right|\,V=\cdot,Z=\cdot\right]
, and is invertible: when
Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{J}
, then
Q_{\mathcal{X}}^{-1}
exists and is unique if and only if
Q\in\mathcal{Q}^{\mathrm{I}}_{J}
with inverse
(Q^{\intercal})^{-1}
viewed as a transposed matrix; more generally, when
Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{\delta}
, then the range-restricted
Q_{\mathcal{X}}:L_{2}(P_{VZ})\to L_{2}(P_{VX})\cap L_{2}(P_{V}\otimes\bar{Q})
and its inverse
Q_{\mathcal{X}}^{-1}:L_{2}(P_{VX})\cap L_{2}(P_{V}\otimes\bar{Q})\to L_{2}(P_{VZ})
are

\displaystyle(Q_{\mathcal{X}}k)(v,x)

\displaystyle=\alpha k(v,x)+(1-\alpha)\int_{\mathfrak{X}}k(v,z)\bar{Q}(\mathrm{d}z),\quad(v,x)\in\mathfrak{V}\times\mathfrak{X},

\displaystyle(Q_{\mathcal{X}}^{-1}h)(v,z)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\alpha}h(v,z)-\frac{1-\alpha}{\alpha}\int_{\mathfrak{X}}h(v,x)\bar{Q}(\mathrm{d}x),\quad(v,z)\in\mathfrak{V}\times\mathfrak{X}.

Theorem 2

(Efficient Influence Function of
\psi(P_{VZ})
)
.

Suppose that
\chi(P_{VX})
has efficient influence function
\varphi\in\overline{\mathrm{lin}\,}\mathcal{T}_{VX}
at
P_{VX}
in some model
\mathcal{P}_{VX}\subset\mathcal{P}_{\mathfrak{V}\mathfrak{X}}
with tangent set
\mathcal{T}_{VX}
, and that
Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{\psi}
. If
\varphi\in Q_{\mathcal{X}}Q_{\mathcal{X}}^{*}\mathcal{T}_{VX}
, then the efficient influence function of
\psi(P_{VZ})
at
P_{VZ}
in the model
\mathcal{P}_{VZ}(Q,\mathcal{P}_{VX})
of (
8
) is
Q_{\mathcal{X}}^{-1}\varphi
. If
\varphi=\tilde{\chi}
in (
4
) in the nonparametric model
\mathcal{P}_{VX}=\mathcal{P}_{\mathfrak{V}\mathfrak{X}}
satisfies
\tilde{\chi}\in L_{2}(P_{VX})\cap L_{2}(P_{V}\otimes\bar{Q})
, then

\displaystyle\tilde{\psi}\coloneqq Q_{\mathcal{X}}^{-1}\tilde{\chi}

(19)

is the efficient influence function of
\psi(P_{VZ})
at
P_{VZ}
in the model
\mathcal{P}_{VZ}(Q,\mathcal{P}_{\mathfrak{V}\mathfrak{X}})
.

In
Theorem

2
, the conditions
\varphi\in Q_{\mathcal{X}}Q_{\mathcal{X}}^{*}\mathcal{T}_{VX}
and
\tilde{\chi}\in L_{2}(P_{VX})\cap L_{2}(P_{V}\otimes\bar{Q})
are important. For instance, if
\bar{Q}
of
Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{\delta}
has large mass at extreme locations of
\tilde{\chi}
, the latter may fail. If they hold, then an asymptotically efficient estimator of
\psi(P_{VZ})
based on a random sample from
P_{VZ}\in\mathcal{P}_{VZ}(Q,

\mathcal{P}_{\mathfrak{V}\mathfrak{X}})
with a given
Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{\psi}
has limiting variance

\displaystyle P_{VZ}\tilde{\psi}^{2}=P_{VX}[Q_{\mathcal{X}}(\tilde{\psi}^{2})]=P_{VX}[Q_{\mathcal{X}}[(Q_{\mathcal{X}}^{-1}\tilde{\chi})(Q_{\mathcal{X}}^{-1}\tilde{\chi})]]

(20)

by the properties of
Q_{\mathcal{X}}
. When there is no privacy, so
Q_{\mathcal{X}}
and
Q_{\mathcal{X}}^{-1}
are the identity, (
20
) equals the nonprivate efficiency bound
P_{VX}\tilde{\chi}^{2}
in
Section

3.2
, as expected. Specifically, if
Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{\delta}
, then we have the bounds

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} P_{VX}\tilde{\chi}^{2}+\frac{1-\alpha}{\alpha}\left((P_{V}\otimes\bar{Q})\tilde{\chi}^{2}-P_{V}\left(\int\tilde{\chi}(V,x)\bar{Q}(\mathrm{d}x)\right)^{2}\right)\leq P_{VZ}\tilde{\psi}^{2}\\
\leq\frac{2-\alpha}{\alpha}P_{VX}\tilde{\chi}^{2}+\frac{2(2-\alpha)(1-\alpha)}{\alpha^{2}}(P_{V}\otimes\bar{Q})\tilde{\chi}^{2}\end{aligned}

(21)

by
Lemma

3
; hence, the private efficiency bound
P_{VZ}\tilde{\psi}^{2}
is never smaller than the nonprivate bound
P_{VX}\tilde{\chi}^{2}
, and the stricter the privacy, the larger this gap in general.

5.  Private Estimation

In this section, we construct a private analogue of the one-step estimator (
1
) of (
T
), and show that it reaches the efficient limit (
20
) in the nonparametric model (
M
) for
P_{VX}
, given a mechanism
Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{\psi}
.
We assume that three, mutually independent, random samples
\bar{\mathcal{S}}=((V_{i},Z_{i}))_{i\in[n]}
,
\bar{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}=((V_{i}^{\prime},Z_{i}^{\prime}))_{i\in[n]},

\bar{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime\prime}=((V_{i}^{\prime\prime},Z_{i}^{\prime\prime}))_{i\in[n]}
from
P_{VZ}
are available for inference.
5
5

5

If
\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{(}(V_{i},X_{i}))_{i\in[n]}
,
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}=((V_{i}^{\prime},X_{i}^{\prime}))_{i\in[n]}
,
\mathcal{S}^{\prime\prime}=((V_{i}^{\prime\prime},X_{i}^{\prime\prime}))_{i\in[n]}
are three, mutually independent, random samples from
P_{VX}
, then, given a
Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{\psi}
, the samples
\bar{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime},\bar{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime\prime}
are obtained by drawing
Z_{i}^{\prime}{|\,}(\mathcal{S},\bar{\mathcal{S}},\mathcal{S}^{\prime},\mathcal{S}^{\prime\prime})\sim Q(\cdot{|\,}X_{i}^{\prime})
and
Z_{i}^{\prime\prime}{|\,}(\mathcal{S},\bar{\mathcal{S}},\mathcal{S}^{\prime},\bar{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime},\mathcal{S}^{\prime\prime})\sim Q(\cdot{|\,}X_{i}^{\prime\prime})
for all
i\in[n]
.

Analogously to (
1
), we begin with an initial estimator
\psi(\hat{P}_{VZ})
and correct it as

\displaystyle\hat{\psi}_{n}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\psi(\hat{P}_{VZ})+\bar{\mathbb{P}}_{n}\hat{\tilde{\psi}}=\psi(\hat{P}_{VZ})+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in[n]}\hat{\tilde{\psi}}(V_{i},Z_{i}),

(22)

where
\bar{\mathbb{P}}_{n}
is the empirical measure of
\bar{\mathcal{S}}
, and

\displaystyle\hat{\tilde{\psi}}\coloneqq

\displaystyle\,Q_{\mathcal{X}}^{-1}\check{\tilde{\chi}},

(23)

\displaystyle\check{\tilde{\chi}}(v,x)\coloneqq

\displaystyle\,\check{r}(v_{\mathit{1}},x)(m(v,x)-{\check{\mu}_{\mathcal{X}}}(v_{\mathit{1}},x))+\frac{\mathbbm{1}_{v_{\mathit{2}}=c}}{\check{p}_{V_{\mathit{2}}}(c)}(g(v,x)-\check{\gamma}_{\mathcal{V}}(c))\check{e}

\displaystyle+f(v,x,{\check{\mu}_{\mathcal{X}}},\check{\gamma}_{\mathcal{V}}(c))-\psi(\hat{P}_{VZ})\eqqcolon\check{\tilde{\chi}}_{0}(v,x)-\psi(\hat{P}_{VZ}).

(24)

are estimates of the private and nonprivate influence functions
\tilde{\psi}
and
\tilde{\chi}
in (
19
) and (
4
), respectively. The inverse
Q_{\mathcal{X}}^{-1}
of (
18
) is known as
Q
is known. As
Q_{\mathcal{X}}^{-1}h=h
for a constant function
h
, we have
\hat{\psi}_{n}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in[n]}(Q_{\mathcal{X}}^{-1}\check{\tilde{\chi}}_{0})(V_{i},Z_{i})
, so
\psi(\hat{P}_{VZ})\in\real
can be arbitrary.

In contrast, all the estimates

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \check{\eta}&\coloneqq(\check{r},{\check{\mu}_{\mathcal{X}}},\check{\gamma}_{\mathcal{V}}(c),\check{p}_{V_{\mathit{2}}}(c),\check{e})\in L_{2}(P_{V_{\mathit{1}}X})\times L_{2}(P_{V_{\mathit{1}}X})\times\Gamma\times\real\times\real\,\text{ of }\\
\eta&\coloneqq(r,\mu_{\mathcal{X}},\gamma_{\mathcal{V}}(c),p_{V_{\mathit{2}}}(c),e)\end{aligned}

(25)

are based on the noisy samples
\bar{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}
and
\bar{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime\prime}
as clarified in
Table

1
. Specifically, by
Lemma

3
,

\displaystyle P_{VZ}Q_{\mathcal{X}}^{-1}h=P_{VX}h\,\text{ for all }h\in L_{2}(P_{VX})\cap L_{2}(P_{V}\otimes\bar{Q}).

(26)

This motivates the estimators

\displaystyle\check{e}\coloneqq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in[n]}\partial_{\gamma}\bar{f}(V_{i}^{\prime\prime},Z_{i}^{\prime\prime},{\check{\mu}_{\mathcal{X}}},\check{\gamma}_{\mathcal{V}}(c)),

(27)

\displaystyle\check{\gamma}_{\mathcal{V}}(c)\coloneqq\frac{1}{\check{p}_{V_{\mathit{2}}}(c)}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in[n]}\bar{g}_{c}(V_{i}^{\prime},Z_{i}^{\prime}),\quad\check{p}_{V_{\mathit{2}}}(c)\coloneqq N_{c}/n,\,N_{c}\coloneqq\sum_{i\in[n]}\mathbbm{1}_{V_{\mathit{2}i}^{\prime}=c},

(28)

\displaystyle\bar{f}(v,z,\mu,\gamma)\coloneqq(Q_{\mathcal{X}}^{-1}(v,x)\mapsto f(v,x,\mu,\gamma))(v,z),\,(v,z,\mu,\gamma)\in\mathfrak{V}\times\mathfrak{Z}\times L_{2}(P_{V_{\mathit{1}}X})\times\Gamma,

(29)

\displaystyle\bar{g}_{c}(v,z)\coloneqq(Q_{\mathcal{X}}^{-1}g_{c})(v,z),\quad g_{c}(v,x)\coloneqq\mathbbm{1}_{v_{\mathit{2}}=c}g(v,x),\quad(v,x,z)\in\mathfrak{V}\times\mathfrak{X}\times\mathfrak{Z}.

(30)

Indeed,
P_{VZ}\partial_{\gamma}\bar{f}=P_{VX}\partial_{\gamma}f
and
\mathbb{E}\check{\gamma}_{\mathcal{V}}(c)=\gamma_{\mathcal{V}}(c)
, by
\partial_{\gamma}\bar{f}=Q_{\mathcal{X}}^{-1}\partial_{\gamma}f
and the definition (
R
) of
\gamma_{\mathcal{V}}(c)
as
\frac{1}{p_{V_{\mathit{2}}}(c)}\mathbb{E}g_{c}(V,X)
. It follows that
\gamma_{\mathcal{V}}(c)
and
p_{V_{\mathit{2}}}(c)
are estimable by (
28
) at rate
O_{{P_{VZ}}}\left(n^{-1/2}\right)
because of their low dimensionality.

The estimation of
(\mu_{\mathcal{X}},r)
is addressed in
Section

6
. If the estimators (
25
) are consistent and
f
is continuous in an appropriate norm accounting for the noise measure
\bar{Q}
in (
16
) as specified in
2
in
Appendix

A
, then the second, empirical process term in the decomposition

\displaystyle\sqrt{n}(\hat{\psi}_{n}-\psi(P_{VZ}))

\displaystyle=\sqrt{n}\bar{\mathbb{P}}_{n}\tilde{\psi}+\sqrt{n}(\bar{\mathbb{P}}_{n}-P_{VZ})(\hat{\tilde{\psi}}-\tilde{\psi})+\sqrt{n}\bar{R}_{n},

(31)

\displaystyle\bar{R}_{n}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\psi(\hat{P}_{VZ})-\psi(P_{VZ})+P_{VZ}\hat{\tilde{\psi}},

(32)

is
o_{P_{VZ}}\left(1\right)
. The first term
\sqrt{n}\bar{\mathbb{P}}_{n}\tilde{\psi}\overset{P_{VZ}}{\rightsquigarrow}

\mathcal{N}(0,P_{VZ}\tilde{\psi}^{2})
by the standard central limit theorem as
\tilde{\psi}\in L_{2}^{0}(P_{VZ})
is the efficient influence function (
19
). The change-of-measure property (
26
) combined with
Theorem

2
and (
24
) implies that the third term

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \bar{R}_{n}=&\ \psi(\hat{P}_{VZ})-\chi(P_{VX})+P_{VX}\check{\tilde{\chi}}\\
=&\ -P_{VX}(r-\check{r})(\mu_{\mathcal{X}}-{\check{\mu}_{\mathcal{X}}})+(\gamma_{\mathcal{V}}(c)-\check{\gamma}_{\mathcal{V}}(c))\left(\frac{p_{V_{\mathit{2}}}(c)}{\check{p}_{V_{\mathit{2}}}(c)}\check{e}-\bar{e}^{\prime}\right)\\
&-(\gamma_{\mathcal{V}}(c)-\check{\gamma}_{\mathcal{V}}(c))^{2}\frac{P_{VX}\partial_{\gamma}^{2}f(V,X,{\check{\mu}_{\mathcal{X}}},\check{\gamma}_{\mathcal{V}}(c))}{2},\end{aligned}

(33)

for some
\tilde{\gamma}_{\mathcal{V}}(c)
between
\gamma_{\mathcal{V}}(c)
and
\check{\gamma}_{\mathcal{V}}(c)
, and

\displaystyle\bar{e}^{\prime}\coloneqq P_{VX}\partial_{\gamma}f(V,X,{\check{\mu}_{\mathcal{X}}},\check{\gamma}_{\mathcal{V}}(c))=P_{VZ}\partial_{\gamma}\bar{f}(V,Z,{\check{\mu}_{\mathcal{X}}},\check{\gamma}_{\mathcal{V}}(c)).

(34)

Whence,
\sqrt{n}\bar{R}_{n}=o_{P_{VZ}}\left(1\right)
under vanishing product
({\check{\mu}_{\mathcal{X}}}-\mu_{\mathcal{X}})(\check{r}-r)
of estimation errors and regularity conditions. This yields our main result whereby
\hat{\psi}_{n}
is rate-double-robust and asymptotically efficient in the nonparametric model (
M
) for
P_{VX}
.

Assumption 1

(Rates of Private Estimators)
.

The estimators (
25
) satisfy
P_{VX}(({\check{\mu}_{\mathcal{X}}}-\mu_{\mathcal{X}})(\check{r}-r))=o_{P_{VZ}}\left(n^{-1/2}\right)
,
\check{\gamma}_{\mathcal{V}}(c)-\gamma_{\mathcal{V}}(c)=O_{{P_{VZ}}}\left(n^{-1/2}\right)
,
\check{p}_{V_{\mathit{2}}}(c)-p_{V_{\mathit{2}}}(c)=O_{{P_{VZ}}}\left(n^{-1/2}\right)
, and
P_{VX}\partial_{\gamma}^{2}f(V,X,{\check{\mu}_{\mathcal{X}}},\tilde{\gamma}_{\mathcal{V}}(c))=O_{{P_{VZ}}}\left(1\right)
for
\tilde{\gamma}_{\mathcal{V}}(c)
between
\check{\gamma}_{\mathcal{V}}(c)
and
\gamma_{\mathcal{V}}(c)
.

Corollary 1

(Asymptotic Efficiency of
\hat{\psi}_{n}
)
.

Suppose that
P_{VZ}\in\mathcal{P}_{VZ}(Q,\mathcal{P}_{\mathfrak{V}\mathfrak{X}})
for a fixed mechanism
Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{\psi}
. If
2
and
1
hold, then

\sqrt{n}(\hat{\psi}_{n}-\psi(P_{VZ}))\overset{P_{VZ}}{\rightsquigarrow}\mathcal{N}(0,P_{VZ}\tilde{\psi}^{2})
as
n\to\infty
.

6.  Private Estimation of Nuisance Parameters

Corollary

1
essentially shows that if the product of the estimation errors of the regression
\mu_{\mathcal{X}}
and of the Riesz representer
r
is small enough, then
\hat{\psi}_{n}
is asymptotically efficient. In this section, we consider the estimation of
(\mu_{\mathcal{X}},r)
from the random sample
\bar{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}
of
Section

5
from
P_{VZ}\in\mathcal{P}_{VZ}(Q,\mathcal{P}_{VX})
given a fixed mechanism
Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{\psi}
.

By the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality, the product is bounded by individual errors as
P_{VX}({\check{\mu}_{\mathcal{X}}}-\mu_{\mathcal{X}})(\check{r}-r)\leq\sqrt{P_{VX}({\check{\mu}_{\mathcal{X}}}-\mu_{\mathcal{X}})^{2}}\sqrt{P_{VX}(\check{r}-r)^{2}}
. If
\mu_{\mathcal{X}}
belongs to a finite-dimensional model smoothly indexed by
\theta\in\real^{K}
, then the product vanishes fast enough for
Corollary

1
to apply under regularity conditions, even if
\check{r}
is an infinite-dimensional nonparametric thus slower estimator, and
vice versa
. To accommodate this tradeoff, we present results for finite- and infinite-dimensional models.

For finite-dimensional smooth models, a common estimator is the method-of-moments (
23
;
33
). In
Proposition

2
, we devise a locally-private method-of-moments estimator enabled by property (
26
), and derive its limiting distribution under the standard regularity conditions of
3
in
Section

D.1
. As in (
21
), the worst-case dependence of the limiting variance
\bar{\Sigma}
on
\alpha
is
1/\alpha^{2}
driven by
\bar{\Phi}
.

Proposition 2

(Private Method-of-Moments)
.

Let
\theta_{0}\coloneqq\arg\min_{\theta\in\Theta}P_{VX}\Xi_{\theta}
for a fixed
\Xi_{\theta}:\mathfrak{V}\times\mathfrak{X}\to\real
,
\theta\in\Theta\subset\real^{K}
, for a fixed
K
. Let
\phi_{\tilde{\theta}}(v,x)\coloneqq\mathrm{D}_{\theta}\Xi_{\tilde{\theta}}(v,x)^{\intercal}
and
\dot{\phi}_{\tilde{\theta}}(v,x)\coloneqq\mathrm{D}_{\theta}\phi_{\tilde{\theta}}(v,x)
be the derivates as maps to
K×1
and to
K×K
, respectively, for
(v,x,\tilde{\theta})\in\mathfrak{V}\times\mathfrak{X}\times\Theta
. Let
\bar{A}_{n}\in\real^{K\times K}
be an arbitrary sequence of (possibly random and then
\sigma(\bar{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime})
-measurable) matrices with
\bar{A}_{n}\overset{P_{VZ}}{\to}\bar{A}_{0}
as
n\to\infty
for a symmetric positive definite
\bar{A}_{0}
, and
\check{\theta}
be the solution to

\theta\mapsto\bar{\Lambda}_{n}(\theta)\coloneqq\big(\bar{\mathbb{P}}_{n}^{\prime}\bar{\phi}_{\theta}^{\intercal}\big)\bar{A}_{n}(\bar{\mathbb{P}}_{n}^{\prime}\bar{\phi}_{\tilde{\theta}}\big)\equiv 0

up to
\bar{\Lambda}_{n}(\check{\theta})=o_{P_{VZ}}\left(n^{-1/2}\right)
, where
\bar{\phi}_{\tilde{\theta}}\coloneqq\mathrm{D}_{\theta}\bar{\Xi}_{\tilde{\theta}}
with
\bar{\Xi}_{\theta}\coloneqq Q_{\mathcal{X}}^{-1}\Xi_{\theta}
for the inverse
Q_{\mathcal{X}}^{-1}
of (
18
). Let
3
in
Section

D.1
hold. If
P_{VZ}\in\mathcal{P}_{VZ}(Q,\mathcal{P}_{VX})
, for a fixed
Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{\psi}
and
P_{VX}\in\mathcal{P}_{VX}
satisfying the given assumptions, then
\sqrt{n}(\check{\theta}-\theta_{0})\overset{P_{VZ}}{\rightsquigarrow}\mathcal{N}(0,\bar{\Sigma})
as
n\to\infty
, where
\bar{\Sigma}\coloneqq({\dot{\Phi}^{\intercal}}\bar{A}_{0}{\dot{\Phi}})^{-1}{\dot{\Phi}^{\intercal}}\bar{A}_{0}\bar{\Phi}\bar{A}_{0}{\dot{\Phi}}({\dot{\Phi}^{\intercal}}\bar{A}_{0}{\dot{\Phi}})^{-1}
,
\dot{\Phi}\coloneqq P_{VX}\dot{\phi}_{\theta_{0}}
,
\bar{\Phi}\coloneqq P_{VZ}\bar{\phi}_{\theta_{0}}\bar{\phi}_{\theta_{0}}^{\intercal}

with
\dot{\Phi}=P_{VX}\dot{\phi}_{\theta_{0}}
and
\phi_{\theta_{0}}=\mathrm{D}_{\theta}\Xi_{\theta_{0}}^{\intercal}
.
Further, let
\xi_{\theta}:\mathfrak{V}\times\mathfrak{X}\to\real
,
\theta\in\Theta
, be (possibly random and then
\sigma(\bar{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime})
-measurable) functions satisfying
3
.
Then
\left\lVert{\xi_{\check{\theta}}-\xi_{\theta_{0}}}\right\rVert_{L_{2}(P_{VX})}=O_{{P_{VZ}}}\left(n^{-1/2}\right)
.

If
\mu_{\mathcal{X}}
or
r
follows a smooth model satisfying the conditions of
Proposition

2
, then they are estimable at parametric rate
O_{{P_{VZ}}}\left(n^{-1/2}\right)
. Indeed, the correctly parametrised versions of the (well-known) relations (
Lemma

8
in
Appendix

D
)

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathcal{X}}=\arg\min_{\mu\in L_{2}(P_{V_{\mathit{1}}}X)}P_{VX}\bigg[\Delta_{\mu}^{2}(V,X)\bigg],\quad\Delta_{\mu}^{2}(v,x)\coloneqq(m(v,x)-\mu(v_{\mathit{1}},x))^{2},

(35)

\displaystyle r_{\gamma}=\arg\min_{h\in L_{2}(P_{V_{\mathit{1}}}X)}P_{VX}\Upsilon_{\gamma,h},\quad\Upsilon_{\gamma,h}(v,x)\coloneqq h(v_{\mathit{1}},x)^{2}-2f(v,x,h,\gamma),\gamma\in\Gamma,

(36)

for the Riesz representer
r_{\gamma}
of
\mu\mapsto\mathbb{E}f(V,X,\mu,\gamma)
, lead to
\left\lVert{\xi_{\check{\theta}}-\xi_{\theta_{0}}}\right\rVert_{L_{2}(P_{VX})}=O_{{P_{VZ}}}\left(n^{-1/2}\right)
for
\xi_{\theta_{0}}\in\{\mu_{\mathcal{X}},r\}
; see
Corollaries

3
and
5
in
Appendix

D
.

For infinite-dimensional models
\theta_{0}\in\{\mu_{\mathcal{X}},r\}
, we transform nonprivate estimators
\hat{\theta}
into private ones for estimation from the noisy data
\bar{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}
. We consider estimators and their private transformation of the form

\displaystyle\hat{\theta}(v_{\mathit{1}},x)\coloneqq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in[n]}w_{n,i}(v_{\mathit{1}},x,V_{i}^{\prime},X_{i}^{\prime},\hat{\vartheta}),

(37)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \check{\theta}(v_{\mathit{1}},x)&\coloneqq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in[n]}\bar{w}_{n,i}(v_{\mathit{1}},x,V_{i}^{\prime},Z_{i}^{\prime},\check{\vartheta}),\\
\bar{w}_{n,i}(v_{\mathit{1}},x,v^{\prime},z^{\prime},\vartheta)&\coloneqq(Q_{\mathcal{X}}^{-1}(v^{\prime},x^{\prime})\mapsto w_{n,i}(v_{\mathit{1}},x,v^{\prime},x^{\prime},\vartheta))(v^{\prime},z^{\prime}),\end{aligned}

(38)

where
w_{n,i}:\mathfrak{V}_{\mathit{1}}\times\mathfrak{X}\times\mathfrak{V}\times\mathfrak{X}\times\mathcal{T}\to\real
,
i\in[n]
, is a triangular array of known, nonrandom functions. Here,
\hat{\vartheta}
estimates
\vartheta\in\mathcal{T}
, which allows
\theta_{0}
to depend on a “secondary-nuisance” parameter
\vartheta
. We remain agnostic about
\mathcal{T}
, affording flexible formulations. The operator
Q_{\mathcal{X}}^{-1}
is the inverse of (
18
), and
\check{\vartheta}
is an estimator of
\vartheta
computed from
\bar{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}
.

Proposition

3
bounds the error of
\check{\theta}
. In (
40
), the first term is the secondary-nuisance error (e.g. the error of the conditioning density in kernel regression estimates), which is trivially zero if there is no dependence on
\vartheta
; the second is a variance term, which usually scales inversely with the effective sample size (e.g.
1/(nh^{d})
for
d
-dimensional kernel estimators with bandwidth
h
) modulo the noise level of the privacy mechanism. The third term in (
40
) is the squared bias, which, thanks to (
26
), is identical to the nonprivate bias (e.g.
h^{2\beta}
for said kernel estimates of
\beta
-smooth parameters). These terms generally require a case-by-case analysis, but arguments in the nonprivate setting can carry over to the private setting under the mechanism
Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{\delta}
. The noise level generally affects the first two terms, but never the third. This can translate into “usual,”
nonprivate
convergence rates of
private
versions of well-known estimators such as kernel or orthogonal series, modulo the noise level of the mechanism
Q
; see
Section

D.2
.

Proposition 3

(Private Error Bounds)
.

Fix a
Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{\psi}
, and let
\check{\theta}
be defined according to (
38
). Suppose that for a sequence of constants
(a_{n})
,

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} P_{VX}T_{n}^{2}&=O_{{P_{VZ}}}\left(a_{n}^{2}\right),\\
T_{n}(v_{\mathit{1}},x)&\coloneqq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in[n]}\left(\bar{w}_{n,i}(v_{\mathit{1}},x,V_{i}^{\prime},Z_{i}^{\prime},\check{\vartheta})-\bar{w}_{n,i}(v_{\mathit{1}},x,V_{i}^{\prime},Z_{i}^{\prime},\vartheta)\right),\quad(v_{\mathit{1}},x)\in\mathfrak{V}_{\mathit{1}}\times\mathfrak{X}.\end{aligned}

(39)

Let
\bar{\sigma}_{i}^{2}(v_{\mathit{1}},x)\coloneqq\Var\left[\bar{w}_{n,i}(v_{\mathit{1}},x,V,Z,\vartheta)\right]
,
(i,v_{\mathit{1}},x)\in[n]\times\mathfrak{V}_{\mathit{1}}\times\mathfrak{X}
. Then for any
\theta\in L_{2}(P_{V_{\mathit{1}}X})
,

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} P_{VX}(\check{\theta}-\theta)^{2}\leq&\,O_{{P_{VZ}}}\left(a_{n}^{2}\right)+O_{{P_{VZ}}}\left(\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i\in[n]}\int\bar{\sigma}_{i}^{2}(v_{\mathit{1}},x)\,\mathrm{d}P_{V_{\mathit{1}}X}(v_{\mathit{1}},x)\right)\\
&+4\int\left\{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in[n]}P_{VX}[w_{n,i}(v_{\mathit{1}},x,V,X,\vartheta)]-\theta(v_{\mathit{1}},x)\right\}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}P_{V_{\mathit{1}}X}(v_{\mathit{1}},x).\end{aligned}

(40)

Morover, if
Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{\delta}
, then

\displaystyle\bar{\sigma}_{i}^{2}(v_{\mathit{1}},x)\leq\frac{4}{\alpha^{2}}\mathbb{E}w_{n,i,}^{2}(v_{\mathit{1}},x,V,Z,\vartheta)

(41)

for all
(i,v_{\mathit{1}},x)\in[n]\times\mathfrak{V}_{\mathit{1}}\times\mathfrak{X}
.

7.  Conclusion

We introduced a class of rate-double-robust target parameters indexed linearly by an infinite-dimensional and nonlinearly by a low-dimensional regression. The inference of these targets was considered in a private setting, when the data — with values in general spaces — of each individual are protected by a local privacy mechanism. We derived the semiparametric properties of the private model, and constructed asymptotically efficient estimators under a fixed privacy mechanism. This efficiency was shown attainable under the same rate-double-robustness condition as in the nonprivate setting, involving two infinite-dimensional parameters. Therefore, we concluded with the private estimation of these two parameters, preserving their nonprivate rates modulo the noise level of the privacy mechanism.

B.1.  Examples

In this section, we present examples of parameters

In

D\in\{0,1\}
and an observed outcome
Y=DY^{1}+(1-D)Y^{0}
for partially unobserved potential outcomes
Y^{0},Y^{1}
with values in . Let

(\mathfrak{X},\mathscr{F}_{\mathfrak{X}})

Y^{d}\!\perp\!\!\!\perp D\mid X
for

. Let

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mu_{\mathcal{X}}(d,x)&\coloneqq\mathbb{E}\left[\left.Y\,\right|\,D=d,X=x\right]\\
\pi_{\mathcal{X}}(d{|\,}x)&\coloneqq\mathbb{E}\left[\left.\mathbbm{1}_{D=d}\,\right|\,X=x\right]\end{aligned}

(52)

for
(d,x)\in\{0,1\}\times\mathfrak{X}

for all

P_{VX}

. In

and

recovers the familiar efficient influence functions for average treatment effects, under the nonparametric model with unknown propensity score. Moreover, the bias formulae in

show the average treatment effect on the treated rate-double-robust. This latter result is aligned with

Example 2

(Average Treatment Effect)
.

In the causal context (
52
), let
V\coloneqq(Y,D)
and
V_{\mathit{1}}\coloneqq D
,

;

,

, and
\gamma_{\mathcal{V}}
are not used. Then
\mathbb{E}Y^{d}=\mathbb{E}\mu_{\mathcal{X}}(d,X)
for

. Hence,
f(V,X,\mu_{\mathcal{X}},\gamma_{\mathcal{V}}(c))\coloneqq\mu_{\mathcal{X}}(d,X)
gives
\chi(P_{VX})=\mathbb{E}Y^{d}
, while

), (

), (

) holding.

The Riesz representer for

is

and for
\mathbb{E}Y^{1}-\mathbb{E}Y^{0}

r(d,x)=\frac{d}{\pi_{\mathcal{X}}(1{|\,}x)}-\frac{1-d}{1-\pi_{\mathcal{X}}(1{|\,}x)}.

None of these representers

depends on
\gamma_{\mathcal{V}}(c)
.

The efficient influence function (
4
), in the nonparametric model (with unknown
\pi_{\mathcal{X}}
), of

, with
\partial_{\gamma}f=0
, is

\displaystyle\tilde{\chi}(\tilde{v},\tilde{x})=\frac{\mathbbm{1}_{\tilde{d}=d}}{\pi_{\mathcal{X}}(d\mid\tilde{x})}(\tilde{y}-\mu_{\mathcal{X}}(d,\tilde{x}))+\mu_{\mathcal{X}}(d,\tilde{x})-\chi(P_{VX});

of
\chi(P_{VX})=\mathbb{E}Y^{1}-\mathbb{E}Y^{0}
, with
\partial_{\gamma}f=0
, is

\displaystyle\tilde{\chi}(v,x)=

\displaystyle\,\frac{d}{\pi_{\mathcal{X}}(1{|\,}x)}(y-\mu_{\mathcal{X}}(1,x))-\frac{1-d}{1-\pi_{\mathcal{X}}(1{|\,}x)}(y-\mu_{\mathcal{X}}(0,x))

\displaystyle+\mu_{\mathcal{X}}(1,X)-\mu_{\mathcal{X}}(0,X)-\chi(P_{VX}),

as in
22
, since
\mathbbm{1}_{\tilde{d}=d}h(\tilde{d},\tilde{x})=\mathbbm{1}_{\tilde{d}=d}h(d,\tilde{x})
for any

. Note that while the model for
(Y^{0},Y^{1},D,

X)
is
not
nonparametric, because it is constrained by

, the model for
(Y,D,X)
is nonparametric if the models for

,

,
D{|\,}X
and
X
are all nonparametric.

Consequently, the bias (
6
) established by
Theorem

1
is

\displaystyle R_{n}\coloneqq

\displaystyle\ -P_{VX}(r-\hat{r})(\mu_{\mathcal{X}}-{\hat{\mu}_{\mathcal{X}}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,P_{DX}\left[\mathbbm{1}_{D=d}\frac{\hat{\pi}_{\mathcal{X}}(d{|\,}X)-\pi_{\mathcal{X}}(d{|\,}X)}{\hat{\pi}_{\mathcal{X}}(d{|\,}X)\pi_{\mathcal{X}}(d{|\,}X)}\big({\hat{\mu}_{\mathcal{X}}}(D,X)-\mu_{\mathcal{X}}(D,X)\big)\right],

for
\mathbb{E}Y^{d}
where
{\hat{\mu}_{\mathcal{X}}},\hat{r}
are some estimators of its Riesz representer and regression, respectively. For
\mathbb{E}Y^{1}-\mathbb{E}Y^{0}

\displaystyle R_{n}\coloneqq-P_{VX}(r-\hat{r})(\mu_{\mathcal{X}}-{\hat{\mu}_{\mathcal{X}}})

\displaystyle=P_{DX}\left[D\frac{\hat{\pi}_{\mathcal{X}}(1{|\,}X)-\pi_{\mathcal{X}}(1{|\,}X)}{\hat{\pi}_{\mathcal{X}}(1{|\,}X)\pi_{\mathcal{X}}(1{|\,}X)}\big({\hat{\mu}_{\mathcal{X}}}(D,X)-\mu_{\mathcal{X}}(D,X)\big)\right]

\displaystyle+P_{DX}\left[(1-D)\frac{\hat{\pi}_{\mathcal{X}}(1{|\,}X)-\pi_{\mathcal{X}}(1{|\,}X)}{(1-\hat{\pi}_{\mathcal{X}}(1{|\,}X))(1-\pi_{\mathcal{X}}(1{|\,}X))}\big({\hat{\mu}_{\mathcal{X}}}(D,X)-\mu_{\mathcal{X}}(D,X)\big)\right]

for the corresponding regression and representer (estimators). By the tower property of expectation conditioning on
X
and by the definition of

, we arrive to the usual bias formulae

\displaystyle R_{n}=

\displaystyle\,P_{X}\left[\frac{\hat{\pi}_{\mathcal{X}}(d{|\,}X)-\pi_{\mathcal{X}}(d{|\,}X)}{\hat{\pi}_{\mathcal{X}}(d{|\,}X)}\big({\hat{\mu}_{\mathcal{X}}}(d,X)-\mu_{\mathcal{X}}(d,X)\big)\right]

\displaystyle\text{ for }\mathbb{E}Y^{d},

\displaystyle R_{n}=

\displaystyle\,P_{X}\left[\frac{\hat{\pi}_{\mathcal{X}}(1{|\,}X)-\pi_{\mathcal{X}}(1{|\,}X)}{\hat{\pi}_{\mathcal{X}}(1{|\,}X)}\big({\hat{\mu}_{\mathcal{X}}}(1,X)-\mu_{\mathcal{X}}(1,X)\big)\right]

\displaystyle+P_{X}\left[\frac{\hat{\pi}_{\mathcal{X}}(1{|\,}X)-\pi_{\mathcal{X}}(1{|\,}X)}{1-\hat{\pi}_{\mathcal{X}}(1{|\,}X)}\big({\hat{\mu}_{\mathcal{X}}}(0,X)-\mu_{\mathcal{X}}(0,X)\big)\right]

\displaystyle\text{ for }\mathbb{E}Y^{1}-\mathbb{E}Y^{0}.

If we did not allow for the dependence on the parameter
\gamma_{\mathcal{V}}
, our class of parameters (

) and conditions (

), (

) would be a strict subset of those of
41
. Allowing for such a nonlinear but smooth dependence enables us to capture more parameters, such as the average treatment effect on the treated, which was indeed shown double robust by
13
.

Example 3

(Average Treatment Effect on the Treated)
.

Consider the setting of
Example

2
, but now take
g(V,X)\coloneqq D
, and
\gamma_{\mathcal{V}}(c)\coloneqq p_{1}
for
p_{1}\coloneqq\mathbb{E}D
;
V_{\mathit{2}}
is again unused and taken to be empty. Then
\mathbb{E}\left[\left.Y^{0}\,\right|\,D=1\right]=\mathbb{E}D\mu_{\mathcal{X}}(0,X)/p_{1}
. Hence
f(V,X,\mu_{\mathcal{X}},\gamma_{\mathcal{V}})\coloneqq D\mu_{\mathcal{X}}(0,X)/p_{1}
gives
\chi(P_{VX})=\mathbb{E}\left[\left.Y^{0}\,\right|\,D=1\right]
, while
f(V,X,\mu_{\mathcal{X}},\gamma_{\mathcal{V}})\coloneqq D(\mu_{\mathcal{X}}(1,X)-\mu_{\mathcal{X}}(0,X))/p_{1}
gives
\chi(P_{VX})=\mathbb{E}\left[\left.Y^{1}-Y^{0}\,\right|\,D=1\right]
,
with (
C.C
), (
C.L
), (
C.D
), (
C.SC
) holding.

The Riesz representer for
\mathbb{E}\left[\left.Y^{0}\,\right|\,D=1\right]
is

r(d,x)=\frac{1-d}{p_{1}}\frac{\pi_{\mathcal{X}}(1{|\,}x)}{1-\pi_{\mathcal{X}}(1{|\,}x)},

and for
\mathbb{E}\left[\left.Y^{1}-Y^{0}\,\right|\,D=1\right]
, it is

r(d,x)=\frac{d}{p_{1}}-\frac{1-d}{p_{1}}\frac{\pi_{\mathcal{X}}(1{|\,}x)}{1-\pi_{\mathcal{X}}(1{|\,}x)}.

Both representers
r
depend on
\gamma_{\mathcal{V}}(c)=p_{1}
.

The efficient influence function (
4
), in the nonparametric model (with unknown
\pi_{\mathcal{X}}
), of
\mathbb{E}\left[\left.Y^{0}\,\right|\,D=1\right]
, with
\partial_{\gamma}f(V,X,\mu_{\mathcal{X}},\gamma_{\mathcal{V}}(c))=-D\mu_{\mathcal{X}}(0,X)/p_{1}^{2}
, is

\displaystyle\tilde{\chi}(v,x)=

\displaystyle\,\frac{1-d}{p_{1}}\frac{\pi_{\mathcal{X}}(1{|\,}x)}{1-\pi_{\mathcal{X}}(1{|\,}x)}(y-\mu_{\mathcal{X}}(d,x))-\frac{d-p_{1}}{p_{1}}\chi(P_{VX})+D\mu_{\mathcal{X}}(0,X)/p_{1}

\displaystyle-\chi(P_{VX})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1-d}{p_{1}}\frac{\pi_{\mathcal{X}}(1{|\,}x)}{1-\pi_{\mathcal{X}}(1{|\,}x)}(y-\mu_{\mathcal{X}}(0,x))+\frac{d}{p_{1}}\mu_{\mathcal{X}}(0,X)-\frac{d}{p_{1}}\chi(P_{VX});

of
\mathbb{E}\left[\left.Y^{1}-Y^{0}\,\right|\,D=1\right]
, with
\partial_{\gamma}f(V,X,\mu_{\mathcal{X}},\gamma_{\mathcal{V}}(c))=-D(\mu_{\mathcal{X}}(1,X)-\mu_{\mathcal{X}}(0,X))/p_{1}^{2}
, is

\displaystyle\tilde{\chi}(v,x)=

\displaystyle\,\left(\frac{d}{p_{1}}-\frac{1-d}{p_{1}}\frac{\pi_{\mathcal{X}}(1{|\,}x)}{1-\pi_{\mathcal{X}}(1{|\,}x)}\right)(y-\mu_{\mathcal{X}}(d,x))-\frac{d-p_{1}}{p_{1}}\chi(P_{VX})

\displaystyle+\frac{d}{p_{1}}(\mu_{\mathcal{X}}(1,x)-\mu_{\mathcal{X}}(0,x))-\chi(P_{VX})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\frac{d}{p_{1}}(y-\mu_{\mathcal{X}}(1,x))-\frac{1-d}{p_{1}}\frac{\pi_{\mathcal{X}}(1{|\,}x)}{1-\pi_{\mathcal{X}}(1{|\,}x)}(y-\mu_{\mathcal{X}}(0,x))

\displaystyle+\frac{d}{p_{1}}(\mu_{\mathcal{X}}(1,x)-\mu_{\mathcal{X}}(0,x))+\frac{d}{p_{1}}\chi(P_{VX})

as in
22
. The remark on the nonparametric nature of the
(Y,D,X)
-model in
Example

2
applies here equally.

Consequently, the first term of the bias in (
106
) established by
Theorem

1
for
\mathbb{E}\left[\left.Y^{0}\,\right|\,D=1\right]
is

\displaystyle-P_{VX}(r-\hat{r})(\mu_{\mathcal{X}}-{\hat{\mu}_{\mathcal{X}}})

\displaystyle=\frac{\hat{p}_{1}-p_{1}}{\hat{p}_{1}p_{1}}P_{X}\left[\frac{(1-\hat{\pi}_{\mathcal{X}}(1{|\,}X))\pi_{\mathcal{X}}(1{|\,}X)}{1-\hat{\pi}_{\mathcal{X}}(1{|\,}X)}({\hat{\mu}_{\mathcal{X}}}(0,X)-\mu_{\mathcal{X}}(0,X))\right]

\displaystyle+\frac{p_{1}}{p_{1}\hat{p}_{1}}P_{X}\left[\frac{(\pi_{\mathcal{X}}(1{|\,}X)-\hat{\pi}_{\mathcal{X}}(1{|\,}X))({\hat{\mu}_{\mathcal{X}}}(0,X)-\mu_{\mathcal{X}}(0,X))}{1-\hat{\pi}_{\mathcal{X}}(1{|\,}X)}\right],

by the tower property of expectation conditioning on
X
; for
\mathbb{E}\left[\left.Y^{1}-Y^{0}\,\right|\,D=1\right]
, it is

\displaystyle-P_{VX}(r-\hat{r})(\mu_{\mathcal{X}}-{\hat{\mu}_{\mathcal{X}}})=\frac{\hat{p}_{1}-p_{1}}{\hat{p}_{1}p_{1}}P_{X}\left[\pi_{\mathcal{X}}(1{|\,}X)({\hat{\mu}_{\mathcal{X}}}(1,X)-\mu_{\mathcal{X}}(1,X))\right]

\displaystyle-\frac{\hat{p}_{1}-p_{1}}{\hat{p}_{1}p_{1}}P_{X}\left[\frac{(1-\hat{\pi}_{\mathcal{X}}(1{|\,}X))\pi_{\mathcal{X}}(1{|\,}X)}{1-\hat{\pi}_{\mathcal{X}}(1{|\,}X)}({\hat{\mu}_{\mathcal{X}}}(0,X)-\mu_{\mathcal{X}}(0,X))\right]

\displaystyle-\frac{p_{1}}{p_{1}\hat{p}_{1}}P_{X}\left[\frac{(\pi_{\mathcal{X}}(1{|\,}X)-\hat{\pi}_{\mathcal{X}}(1{|\,}X))({\hat{\mu}_{\mathcal{X}}}(0,X)-\mu_{\mathcal{X}}(0,X))}{1-\hat{\pi}_{\mathcal{X}}(1{|\,}X)}\right].

Suppose
\hat{e}-e^{\prime}=o_{P_{VX}}\left(1\right)
and that
P_{YDX}\partial_{\gamma}^{2}f(Y,D,X,{\hat{\mu}_{\mathcal{X}}},\tilde{p}_{1})=O_{{P_{VX}}}\left(1\right)
. Then for the bias to vanish as
o_{P_{VX}}\left(n^{-1/2}\right)
, it suffices that
\left\lVert{\Delta}\right\rVert_{L_{1}(P_{VX})}=o_{P_{VX}}\left(n^{-1/2}\right)
, where
\Delta(x)\coloneqq({\hat{\mu}_{\mathcal{X}}}(0{|\,}x)-\mu_{\mathcal{X}}(0{|\,}x))(\pi_{\mathcal{X}}(1{|\,}x)-\hat{\pi}_{\mathcal{X}}(1{|\,}x))
, and
{\hat{\mu}_{\mathcal{X}}}
be consistent because
\hat{p}_{1}-p_{1}=O_{{P_{VX}}}\left(n^{-1/2}\right)
, provided
1-\hat{\pi}_{\mathcal{X}}(1{|\,}x)
is bounded away from zero.

Parameters with a more geometric interpretation are also included in our class.

Example 4

(Average Approximate Derivative)
.

Let
\mathfrak{X}=\real
, and
V\coloneqq Y
for a random variable
Y
. Set
m(V,X)\coloneqq Y
, and assume that
\mu_{\mathcal{X}}(v_{\mathit{1}},x)=\mathbb{E}\left[\left.Y\,\right|\,V_{\mathit{1}}=v_{\mathit{1}},X=x\right]
does not depend on
v_{\mathit{1}}
, so we use
\mu_{\mathcal{X}}(x)
to refer to its value. Assume that the marginal distribution
P_{X}
of
X
admits a Lebesque density
p_{X}
.

Fix a strictly positive constant
\epsilon\in\real
. Setting
f(v,x,\mu,\gamma)\coloneqq\frac{\mu(x+\epsilon)-\mu(x)}{\epsilon}
and
J_{P_{VX},\epsilon}(\mu)\coloneqq\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\mu(X+\epsilon)-\mu(X)}{\epsilon}\right]
,
\mu\in L_{2}(P_{X})
, yields the parameter

\displaystyle\chi(P_{VX})=J_{P_{VX},\epsilon}(\mu_{\mathcal{X}})=\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\mu_{\mathcal{X}}(X+\epsilon)-\mu_{\mathcal{X}}(X)}{\epsilon}\right]=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\mu_{\mathcal{X}}(x+\epsilon)-\mu_{\mathcal{X}}(x)}{\epsilon}p_{X}(x)\mathrm{d}x.

For small
\epsilon
, the integrand represents an approximate derivative of
\mu_{\mathcal{X}}
, although we do not actually require that
\mu_{\mathcal{X}}
be differentiable, unlike
13
.

Assume that
(P_{VX},\epsilon)
satisfies

\displaystyle b(P_{VX},\epsilon)\coloneqq\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\left(\frac{p_{X}(x-\epsilon)}{p_{X}(x)}\right)^{2}p_{X}(x)\mathrm{d}x<\infty.

(53)

Then
J_{P_{VX},\epsilon}
has Riesz representer
r(x)=\frac{p_{X}(x-\epsilon)-p_{X}(x)}{\epsilon p_{X}(x)}
, whose dependence on
(P_{VX},\epsilon)
is suppressed in notation. The last display ensures that
J_{P_{VX},\epsilon}
is continuous so that the Riesz representation theorem applies. Indeed, by a change of variables, and the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality, for
\mu,\tilde{\mu}\in L_{2}(P_{X})
,

\displaystyle|J_{P_{VX},\epsilon}(\mu)-J_{P_{VX},\epsilon}(\tilde{\mu})|\leq\mathbb{E}\left[|\mu(X)-\tilde{\mu}(X)|\frac{p_{X}(X-\epsilon)}{p_{X}(X)}\right]+\left\lVert{\mu-\tilde{\mu}}\right\rVert_{L_{2}(P_{X})}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\sqrt{b(P_{VX},\epsilon)}+1\right)\left\lVert{\mu-\tilde{\mu}}\right\rVert_{L_{2}(P_{X})}.

Hence, (
C.C
), (
C.L
), and (
C.D
) hold. In addition, if
(P_{VX},\epsilon)
satisfies
\left\lVert{\frac{p_{X}(\cdot-\epsilon)}{p_{X}(\cdot)}}\right\rVert_{\infty}<\infty
— which is stronger than (
53
) — , then (
C.SC
) holds by similar arguments.

Example 5

(Ray-Type Integral)
.

Let
\mathfrak{X}=\real^{d}
, and
V\coloneqq(Y,W)
, for a random variable
Y
, and a random vector
W
with values
d
and Lebesgue density
p_{W}
. Set
m(V,X)\coloneqq Y
, and assume that
\mu_{\mathcal{X}}(v_{\mathit{1}},x)\equiv\mu_{\mathcal{X}}(x)
does not depend on
v_{\mathit{1}}
. Suppose that
P_{X}
has a Lebesgue density
p_{X}
.

Fix constants
0<t_{0}<t_{1}
. Setting
f(v,x,\mu,\gamma)\coloneqq\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\mu(tw)\left\lVert{w}\right\rVert_{2}\,\mathrm{d}t
and
J_{P_{VX},t_{0},t_{1}}(\mu)\coloneqq\mathbb{E}\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\mu(tW)\left\lVert{W}\right\rVert_{2}\,\mathrm{d}t
,
\mu\in L_{2}(P_{X})
, where
\left\lVert{w}\right\rVert_{2}=\sqrt{\sum_{j=1}^{d}w_{j}^{2}}
is the Euclidean norm on
d
, yields the parameter

\displaystyle\chi(P_{VX})\coloneqq J_{P_{VX},t_{0},t_{1}}(\mu_{\mathcal{X}})=\mathbb{E}\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\mu_{\mathcal{X}}(tW)\left\lVert{W}\right\rVert_{2}\,\mathrm{d}t=\int_{\real{}^{d}}\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\mu_{\mathcal{X}}(tw)\left\lVert{w}\right\rVert_{2}\,\mathrm{d}tp_{W}(w)\,\mathrm{d}w.

The quantity
f(v,x,\mu,\gamma)
is the integral of
\mu
along the curve
\zeta_{w}:[t_{0},t_{1}]\to\real^{d}
,
\zeta_{w}(t)\coloneqq tw
, the line segment between
t_{0}w
and
t_{1}w
, and
J_{P_{VX},t_{0},t_{1}}(\mu)
is its average across draws from
p_{W}
. With
t_{0}
close to zero and
t_{1}
to infinity,
f(v,x,\mu,\gamma)
approximates the ray emanating from the origin in the direction of
w
, but care must be taken to ensure integrability: if
(P_{VX},t_{0},t_{1})
is such that

\real{}^{d}\ni x\mapsto\frac{\left\lVert{x}\right\rVert_{2}}{p_{X}(x)}\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}p_{W}\left(\frac{x}{t}\right)\frac{1}{t^{d+1}}\,\mathrm{d}t\in L_{2}(P_{X}),

then the map in the display is the Riesz representer of
J_{P_{VX},t_{0},t_{1}}
, with (
C.C
) and (
C.L
) holding. (Condition (
C.D
) is trivially satisfied.) This follows from

\displaystyle J_{P_{VX},t_{0},t_{1}}(\mu)=\int_{\real{}^{d}}\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\mu(tw)\left\lVert{w}\right\rVert_{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\,p_{W}(w)\,\mathrm{d}w=\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\int_{\real{}^{d}}\mu(u)\left\lVert{u/t}\right\rVert_{2}p_{W}(u/t)\frac{1}{t^{d}}\,\mathrm{d}u\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{\real{}^{d}}\mu(u)\frac{\left\lVert{u}\right\rVert_{2}}{p_{X}(u)}\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}p_{W}(u/t)\frac{1}{t^{d+1}}\,\mathrm{d}tp_{X}(u)\,\mathrm{d}u=\mathbb{E}\mu(X)\frac{\left\lVert{X}\right\rVert_{2}}{p_{X}(X)}\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}p_{W}(X/t)\frac{1}{t^{d+1}}\,\mathrm{d}t,

where the second equality is by a change of variables
(wt,t)\mapsto(u,t)
with a Jacobian whose determinant is
t^{-d}
.

Example 6

(Line Integral)
.

Instead of taking an integral along a ray as in
Example

5
, we can integrate along the curve
\zeta_{w_{\mathrm{b}},w_{\mathrm{e}}}:[0,1]\to\real^{d}
,
\zeta_{w_{\mathrm{b}},w_{\mathrm{e}}}(t)\coloneqq(1-t)w_{\mathrm{b}}+tw_{\mathrm{e}}
, which is the straight line connecting
w_{\mathrm{b}}
and
w_{\mathrm{e}}
in
d
.

Let
\mathfrak{X}=\real^{d}
, and
V\coloneqq(Y,W)
, for a random variable
Y
, and a random vector
W=(W_{\mathrm{b}},W_{\mathrm{e}})
with values
\real{}^{d}\times\real^{d}
and Lebesgue density
p_{W}
. Set
m(V,X)\coloneqq Y
, and assume that
\mu_{\mathcal{X}}(v_{\mathit{1}},x)\equiv\mu_{\mathcal{X}}(x)
does not depend on
v_{\mathit{1}}
. Suppose that
P_{X}
has a Lebesgue density
p_{X}
. Setting
f(v,x,\mu,\gamma)\coloneqq\int_{0}^{1}\mu((1-t)w_{\mathrm{b}}+tw_{\mathrm{e}})\left\lVert{w_{\mathrm{e}}-w_{\mathrm{b}}}\right\rVert_{2}\,\mathrm{d}t
and
J_{P_{VX}}(\mu)=\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{1}\mu((1-t)W_{\mathrm{b}}+tW_{\mathrm{e}})\left\lVert{W_{\mathrm{e}}-W_{\mathrm{b}}}\right\rVert_{2}\,\mathrm{d}t
yields the parameter

\displaystyle\chi(P_{VX})=J_{P_{VX}}(\mu_{\mathcal{X}})=\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{1}\mu_{\mathcal{X}}((1-t)W_{\mathrm{b}}+tW_{\mathrm{e}})\left\lVert{W_{\mathrm{e}}-W_{\mathrm{b}}}\right\rVert_{2}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{\real{}^{d}\times\real^{d}}\int_{0}^{1}\mu_{\mathcal{X}}((1-t)w_{\mathrm{b}}+tw_{\mathrm{e}})\left\lVert{w_{\mathrm{e}}-w_{\mathrm{b}}}\right\rVert_{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\,p_{W}(w_{\mathrm{b}},w_{\mathrm{e}})\,\mathrm{d}w_{\mathrm{b}}\,\mathrm{d}w_{\mathrm{e}}.

This is the average line integral of
\mu_{\mathcal{X}}
across draws of random endpoints
(W_{\mathrm{b}},W_{\mathrm{e}})
from
p_{W}
. If
P_{VX}
is such that

\real{}^{d}\ni x\mapsto\frac{1}{p_{X}(x)}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\real{}^{d}}\left\lVert{\Delta}\right\rVert_{2}p_{W}(x-t\Delta,x+(1-t)\Delta)\,\mathrm{d}\Delta\,\mathrm{d}t\in L_{2}(P_{X}),

then the map in the display is the Riesz representer of
J_{P_{VX}}
. This follows from

\displaystyle\int_{\real{}^{d}\times\real^{d}}\int_{0}^{1}\mu((1-t)w_{\mathrm{b}}+tw_{\mathrm{e}})\left\lVert{w_{\mathrm{e}}-w_{\mathrm{b}}}\right\rVert_{2}\,\mathrm{d}tp_{W}(w_{\mathrm{b}},w_{\mathrm{e}})\,\mathrm{d}w_{\mathrm{b}}\,\mathrm{d}w_{\mathrm{e}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\real{}^{d}\times\real^{d}}\int_{0}^{1}\mu(u)\left\lVert{\Delta}\right\rVert_{2}p_{W}(u-t\Delta,u+(1-t)\Delta)|\mathrm{det}J(u,\Delta,t)|\,\mathrm{d}t\,\mathrm{d}\Delta\,\mathrm{d}u,

by a change of variables

(w_{\mathrm{b}},w_{\mathrm{e}},t)\mapsto((1-t)w_{\mathrm{b}}+tw_{\mathrm{e}},w_{\mathrm{e}}-w_{\mathrm{b}},t)\eqqcolon(u,\Delta,t)\in\real^{d}\times\real^{d}\times[0,1].

Above,
\mathrm{det}J(u,\Delta,t)
is the determinant of the Jacobian matrix, in
(2d+1)×(2d+1)
, of
(u,\Delta,t)\mapsto(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3})(u,\Delta,t)

\coloneqq(u-t\Delta,u+(1-t)\Delta,t)=(w_{\mathrm{b}},w_{\mathrm{e}},t)
, which is

\displaystyle J(u,\Delta,t)=\begin{bmatrix}\mathrm{D}_{u}g_{1}&\mathrm{D}_{\Delta}g_{1}&\mathrm{D}_{t}g_{1}\\
\mathrm{D}_{u}g_{2}&\mathrm{D}_{\Delta}g_{2}&\mathrm{D}_{t}g_{2}\\
\mathrm{D}_{u}g_{3}&\mathrm{D}_{\Delta}g_{3}&\mathrm{D}_{t}g_{3}\end{bmatrix}(u,\Delta,t)=\begin{bmatrix}\mathrm{I}_{d}&-t\mathrm{I}_{d}&-\Delta\\
\mathrm{I}_{d}&(1-t)\mathrm{I}_{d}&-\Delta\\
0_{d}^{\intercal}&0_{d}^{\intercal}&1\\
\end{bmatrix},

where
\mathrm{I}_{d}
is the identity matrix in
d
, and
0_{d}^{\intercal}\in\real^{1\times d}
is the transposed zero vector. Hence,
|\mathrm{det}J(u,\Delta,t)|=|\mathrm{det}\begin{bmatrix}\mathrm{I}_{d}&-t\mathrm{I}_{d}\\
\mathrm{I}_{d}&(1-t)\mathrm{I}_{d}\end{bmatrix}|=1
,
because of how the determinant of block matrices is computed.

While not pursued here, these arguments could be extended to higher order Bézier curves with random control points
W_{1},\ldots,W_{k}
, or other curves indeed.

A special subset of our class is a set of parameters with known Riesz representers. Their estimators admit favourable efficiency properties.

Example 7

(Known Riesz Representer)
.

In the general context of
Section

3
, suppose that
f(v,x,\mu,\gamma)=\mu(v_{\mathit{1}},x)b(v_{\mathit{1}},x)
, independent of
\gamma
, for a
known
function
b:\mathfrak{V}_{\mathit{1}}\times\mathfrak{X}\to\real
. If
b\in L_{2}(P_{V_{\mathit{1}}X})
, then, trivially,
b
is the Riesz representer of
L_{2}(P_{V_{\mathit{1}}X})\ni\mu\mapsto\mathbb{E}\mu(V_{\mathit{1}},X)b(V_{\mathit{1}},X)
.

In this case,
1
is automatically satisfied, and asymptotic efficiency as per
Corollary

1
follows merely under the consistency requirements of
2
for an estimator of
\mu_{\mathcal{X}}
.

Situations with a known Riesz representer may exist:

Example 8

(Time Discounting)
.

Consider a simple economics model with two time periods. Furthest in the future, in Period 2, a stock value
Y\geq 0
is going to be revealed. In Period 1, a random element
X
is realised, which potentially helps predict the stock value through
\mu_{\mathcal{X}}(x)\coloneqq\mathbb{E}\left[\left.Y\,\right|\,X=x\right]
. In Period 1, an agent owns a stock, and signs a binding contract — after
X=x
is realised — that she would sell the stock at price
\mu_{\mathcal{X}}(x)
in Period 2. (This is a “fair bet,” as her
a priori
expected profit given
X=x
,
\mathbb{E}\left[\left.\mu_{\mathcal{X}}(x)-Y\,\right|\,X=x\right]
, is zero.)

Viewed from Period 1, the present value of the agent’s Period-2 income is
\frac{1}{1+\kappa(x)}\mu_{\mathcal{X}}(x)
, where
\kappa:\mathfrak{X}\to[0,\infty)
is a discount rate, a
nonrandom, known
function; the larger the
\kappa
, the more impatient she is. The element
X
can be thought of as all relevant information available in Period 1.

The parameter

\displaystyle\chi(P_{VX})\coloneqq\mathbb{E}\frac{1}{1+\kappa(X)}\mu_{\mathcal{X}}(X)

is the expected (before
X
is observed) present value of the agent’s Period-2
income
viewed from Period 1. (The expected present value of her
profit
,
\mathbb{E}\frac{1}{1+\kappa(X)}(\mu_{\mathcal{X}}(X)-Y)
, is zero.) It is of the form (
T
) for
V\coloneqq Y
and
m(V,X)=Y
.

As
\kappa\geq 0
,
x\mapsto\frac{1}{1+\kappa(x)}\in L_{2}(P_{X})
is the Riesz-representer of
\mu\mapsto\mathbb{E}\frac{1}{1+\kappa(X)}\mu(X)
, and (
C.C
) and (
C.L
) both hold. This is an instance of a known Riesz representer in
Example

7
. Consequently, a merely consistent estimator of
\mu_{\mathcal{X}}
suffices for asymptotically efficient estimation of
\chi(P_{VX})
. This is attractive as it affords “complicated” predictors
X
, at essentially no cost (although the asymptotic variance
P_{VZ}\tilde{\psi}^{2}
may change with different choices of
X
).

We may extend our class to be indexed by multiple regressions.

Example 9

(Multiple Regressions)
.

The parameter class (
T
) in
Section

3
can easily be extended to feature linear dependence on multiple infinite-dimensional regressions

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mu_{\mathcal{X},1}(v_{\mathit{1}},x)&\coloneqq\mathbb{E}\left[\left.m_{1}(V,X)\,\right|\,V_{\mathit{1}}=v_{\mathit{1}},X=x\right],\\
\vdots&\\
\mu_{\mathcal{X},K}(v_{\mathit{1}},x)&\coloneqq\mathbb{E}\left[\left.m_{K}(V,X)\,\right|\,V_{\mathit{1}}=v_{\mathit{1}},X=x\right],\end{aligned}

(54)

where the
m_{k}:\mathfrak{V}\times\mathfrak{X}\to\real
. Indeed,
H_{K}\coloneqq(L_{2}(P_{V_{\mathit{1}}X}))^{K}
is a Hilbert space with inner product
\langle\mu,\nu\rangle_{H_{K}}\coloneqq\sum_{k=1}^{K}\langle\mu_{k},\nu_{k}\rangle_{L_{2}(P_{V_{\mathit{1}}X})}=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\int\mu_{k}\nu_{k}\,\mathrm{d}P_{V_{\mathit{1}}X}
. Then we define
f:\mathfrak{V}\times\mathfrak{X}\times H_{K}\times\Gamma\to\real
requiring the linearity of
H_{K}\ni\mu\mapsto f(V,X,\mu,\gamma)

P_{VX}
-almost surely, for all
\gamma\in\Gamma
(meaning that for all
\beta\in\real
,
f(V,X,(\beta\mu_{1},\ldots,\beta\mu_{K}),\gamma)=\beta f(V,X,(\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{K}),\gamma)
,
P_{VX}
-almost surely, for all
\gamma\in\Gamma
), and the continuity of
H_{K}\ni\mu\mapsto P_{VX}f(V,X,\mu,\gamma)
for all
\gamma\in\Gamma
for the distance
\left\lVert{\mu-\nu}\right\rVert_{H_{K}}\coloneqq\sqrt{\langle\mu-\nu,\mu-\nu\rangle_{H_{K}}}=\sqrt{\sum_{k=1}^{K}\int(\mu_{k}-\nu_{k})^{2}\,\mathrm{d}P_{V_{\mathit{1}}X}}
. Then the Riesz representation theorem applies equally: for all
(P_{VX},\gamma)
there exists a unique element
r_{\gamma}=(r_{\gamma,1},\ldots,r_{\gamma,K})\in H_{K}
such that

\displaystyle P_{VX}f(V,X,\mu,\gamma)=\sum_{k=1}^{K}P_{V_{\mathit{1}}X}(r_{\gamma,k}\mu_{k})\quad\text{ for all }\mu\in H_{K}.

This yields results very similar to the single-infinite-dimensional-regression case.

Indeed, let
\mu_{\mathcal{X}}\coloneqq(\mu_{\mathcal{X},1},\ldots,\mu_{\mathcal{X},K})\in H_{K}
. Then efficient influence function of
\chi(P_{VX})\coloneqq P_{VX}f(V,X,\mu_{\mathcal{X}},\gamma_{\mathcal{V}}(c))
, in
Proposition

1
becomes

\displaystyle\tilde{\chi}(v,x)=

\displaystyle\,\sum_{k=1}^{K}r_{k}(v_{\mathit{1}},x)(m_{k}(v,x)-\mu_{\mathcal{X},k}(v_{\mathit{1}},x))

\displaystyle+\frac{\mathbbm{1}_{v_{\mathit{2}}=c}}{p_{V_{\mathit{2}}}(c)}(g(v,x)-\gamma_{\mathcal{V}}(c))\mathbb{E}\partial_{\gamma}f(V,X,\mu_{\mathcal{X}},\gamma_{\mathcal{V}}(c))

\displaystyle+f(v,x,\mu_{\mathcal{X}},\gamma_{\mathcal{V}}(c))-\chi(P_{VX}),

where
r=(r_{1},\ldots,r_{K})\in H_{K}
is the Riesz representer of
H_{K}\ni\mu\mapsto P_{VX}f(V,X,\mu,\gamma_{\mathcal{V}}(c))
.

Correspondingly, the rate-double-robustness property of
Theorem

1
becomes

\displaystyle\chi^{\prime}-\chi(P_{VX})+P_{VX}\tilde{\chi}^{\prime}=

\displaystyle\,\sum_{k=1}^{K}P_{VX}(r_{k}^{\prime}-r_{k})(\mu_{\mathcal{X},k}-\mu_{\mathcal{X},k}^{\prime})

\displaystyle+(\gamma_{\mathcal{V}}(c)-\gamma_{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}(c))\left(\frac{p_{V_{\mathit{2}}}(c)}{p_{V_{\mathit{2}}}^{\prime}(c)}e^{\prime\prime}-e^{\prime}\right)

\displaystyle-(\gamma_{\mathcal{V}}(c)-\gamma_{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}(c))^{2}\frac{P_{VX}\partial_{\gamma}^{2}f(V,X,\mu_{\mathcal{X}}^{\prime},\widetilde{\gamma_{\mathcal{V}}(c)})}{2},

with

\displaystyle\tilde{\chi}^{\prime}(v,x)\coloneqq

\displaystyle\,\sum_{k=1}^{K}r_{k}^{\prime}(v_{\mathit{1}},x)(m_{k}(v,x)-\mu_{\mathcal{X},k}^{\prime}(v_{\mathit{1}},x))+\frac{\mathbbm{1}_{v_{\mathit{2}}=c}}{p_{V_{\mathit{2}}}^{\prime}(c)}(g(v,x)-\gamma_{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}(c))e^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle+f(v,x,\mu_{\mathcal{X}}^{\prime},\gamma_{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}(c))-\chi^{\prime},

where the
r_{k}^{\prime},\mu_{\mathcal{X},k}^{\prime}
are both arbitrary elements of
L_{2}(P_{V_{\mathit{1}}X})
with
\mu_{\mathcal{X}}^{\prime}\coloneqq(\mu_{\mathcal{X},1}^{\prime},\ldots,\mu_{\mathcal{X},K}^{\prime})
;
p_{V_{\mathit{2}}}^{\prime}(c),\gamma_{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}(c),\chi^{\prime},e^{\prime\prime}\in\real
are arbitrary;
e^{\prime}\coloneqq P_{VX}\partial_{\gamma}f(V,X,\mu_{\mathcal{X}}^{\prime},\gamma_{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}(c));
and
\widetilde{\gamma_{\mathcal{V}}(c)}
is some value between
\gamma_{\mathcal{V}}(c)
and
\gamma_{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}(c)
. That is, the rate-double-robustness property holds, with pairwise rate-tradeoffs between the
r_{k}^{\prime}
and
\mu_{\mathcal{X},k}^{\prime}
.

Moreover, if
\mathfrak{V}_{1}=\mathfrak{V}_{\mathit{1},1}\times\ldots\times\mathfrak{V}_{\mathit{1},L}
and
\mathfrak{X}=\mathfrak{X}_{1}\times\ldots\mathfrak{X}_{K}
, then we could generalise (
54
) further by defining
L\times K
regressions

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathcal{X},l,k}(v_{\mathit{1},l},x_{k})\coloneqq\mathbb{E}\left[\left.m_{lk}(V,X)\,\right|\,V_{\mathit{1},l}=v_{\mathit{1},l},X_{k}=x_{k}\right],\,(v_{\mathit{1},l},x_{k})\in\mathfrak{V}_{\mathit{1},l}\times\mathfrak{X}_{k},

with
m_{lk}:\mathfrak{V}\times\mathfrak{X}\to\real
, for
(l,k)\in[L]\times[K]
, and by considering the Hilbert space
H\coloneqq\otimes_{l=1}^{L}\otimes_{k=1}^{K}L_{2}(P_{V_{\mathit{1},l}X_{k}})
with the inner product
\langle\mu,\nu\rangle_{H}\coloneqq\sum_{l=1}^{L}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\langle\mu_{l,k},\nu_{l,k}\rangle_{L_{2}(P_{V_{\mathit{1},l}X_{k}})}=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\int\mu_{l,k}\nu_{l,k}\,\mathrm{d}P_{V_{\mathit{1},l}X_{k}}
. The efficient influence function and the rate-double-robustness would be preserved with the obvious modifications.

Finally, we remark that for
\mathfrak{V}_{\mathit{2}}=\mathfrak{V}_{\mathit{2},1}\times\ldots\times\mathfrak{V}_{\mathit{2},K}
, dependence on multiple low-dimensional regressions

\displaystyle\gamma_{\mathcal{V},k}(v_{\mathit{2}})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathbb{E}\left[\left.g_{k}(V,X)\,\right|\,V_{\mathit{2},k}=v_{\mathit{2},k}\right],\quad v_{\mathit{2},k}\in\mathfrak{V}_{\mathit{2},k},\,\,g_{k}:\mathfrak{V}\times\mathfrak{X}\to\real,\,\,k\in[K],

can be accommodated as well. This entails a vector-valued derivative
\partial_{\gamma}f
and corresponding vector
e
, with number of entries determined by the
|\mathfrak{V}_{\mathit{2},k}|
.

B.2.  Proofs

This section proves
Propositions

1
and
1
in
Section

3
.

We first note that (
C.C
), (
C.L
) imply that for all
\gamma\in\Gamma
, there exists a unique function
r_{P_{VX},\gamma}:\mathfrak{V}_{\mathit{1}}\times\mathfrak{X}\to\real
,
r_{P_{VX},\gamma}\in L_{2}(P_{V_{\mathit{1}}X})
, such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}f(V,X,\mu,\gamma)=\mathbb{E}r_{P_{VX},\gamma}(V_{\mathit{1}},X)\mu(V_{\mathit{1}},X)\quad\text{ for all }\mu\in L_{2}(P_{V_{\mathit{1}}X}),

where the expectations are taken with respect to
P_{VX}
. This is the consequence of the Riesz representation theorem, and
r_{\gamma,P_{VX}}
is called the Riesz representer of
\mu\mapsto\mathbb{E}f(V,X,\mu,\gamma)
.

Proof of
Proposition

1
.

First, note that by the definition of
\mu_{\mathcal{X}},\gamma_{\mathcal{V}}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}r(V_{\mathit{1}},X)(m(V,X)-\mu_{\mathcal{X}}(V_{\mathit{1}},X))

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\mathbb{E}\left[\left.r(V_{\mathit{1}},X)(m(V,X)-\mu_{\mathcal{X}}(V_{\mathit{1}},X))\,\right|\,V_{\mathit{1}},X\right]
